The paper introduces BOE-XSUM, a new Spanish dataset for extreme summarization of legal documents from the "Boletín Oficial del Estado" (BOE), Spain's official state gazette. The dataset contains 3,648 examples, each consisting of a concise, plain-language summary of a BOE entry, the original BOE text, and a document type label. The authors evaluate the performance of fine-tuned medium-sized large language models (LLMs) on BOE-XSUM, comparing them to general-purpose generative models in a zero-shot setting. The results demonstrate that fine-tuned models outperform their non-specialized counterparts, with BERTIN GPT-J 6B achieving a 24% performance gain over DeepSeek-R1. The dataset is intended to bridge the gap between complex legal language and citizen understanding.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the dataset itself. While summarization is a well-trodden research area, the creation of a **high-quality, curated dataset specifically for extreme summarization of Spanish legal texts** is a significant contribution. The focus on transforming complex legal language into clear language makes it particularly relevant and addresses a real-world need. Existing datasets for Spanish summarization are either scarce or do not target this specific domain and summarization style. The use of journalistic summaries as a basis further adds to the pragmatic value. However, there's little algorithmic novelty. The paper uses standard fine-tuning techniques.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the practical applicability of the dataset. It provides a valuable resource for:

    *   **Training models:** Develop models capable of generating easily understandable summaries of legal documents, improving citizen access to important information.

    *   **Benchmarking:** Offering a benchmark dataset for evaluating the performance of summarization models on this challenging domain.

    *   **Improving Legal Understanding:** Aiding in making public administrations more accessible.

    *   The **rigor is adequate**, with careful curation, data cleaning, and initial experimentation. However, further experimentation with various training strategies and advanced evaluation metrics tailored to the specific demands of this task would strengthen the research.
    *   The paper would be even stronger if it explored the broader societal impact by analyzing the impact of these types of summaries and their use-cases.
    *   The **content analysis could be improved** by providing more examples of how and why certain decisions were made when creating these datasets.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The evaluation is mainly based on standard automatic metrics (BLEU, ROUGE, METEOR, BERTScore). While these metrics provide a general indication of performance, they might not fully capture the nuances of "clarity" or "usefulness," especially in the context of legal language. A more thorough human evaluation would be beneficial. The current human evaluation is brief and only focuses on consistency.
    *   The experiments are limited to fine-tuning one specific model architecture (BERTIN GPT-J 6B). Exploring other models and fine-tuning approaches could provide a more comprehensive analysis.  Although the paper makes brief mention of GPT-4 and DeepSeek in a zero-shot setting.
    *   The dataset size, while respectable, could be a limitation. Larger datasets generally lead to better-performing models. Future work could involve expanding the dataset.
    *   The prompt engineering is only limited to one prompt design. Further prompt engineering, A/B tests and testing with various prompt designs could show the importance of high quality prompts to improve the models responses.
    *   There's limited discussion about potential biases present in the dataset. Given that the summaries are based on journalist selection, there's a possibility of certain topics being over-represented.
    *   The paper could benefit from in-depth case-studies to show particular areas of great success or great failure.

*   **Potential Influence:** The BOE-XSUM dataset has the potential to stimulate further research in Spanish NLP, particularly in the areas of legal summarization and language accessibility. It could also serve as a model for creating similar datasets in other languages and domains.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, a score of **7** is assigned. The creation of the BOE-XSUM dataset is a valuable and timely contribution. It has the potential to advance research in a practically relevant area. However, the limited evaluation metrics and experimental scope, as well as the potential for more thorough analysis of the data and model performance, hold it back from being a truly exceptional contribution. The paper would be enhanced by more diverse evaluations.

### When Scores Learn Geometry: Rate Separations under the Manifold Hypothesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24912v1)
- **Authors**: Xiang Li, Zebang Shen, Ya-Ping Hsieh, Niao He
- **Abstract**: Score-based methods, such as diffusion models and Bayesian inverse problems, are often interpreted as learning the data distribution in the low-noise limit ($\sigma \to 0$). In this work, we propose an alternative perspective: their success arises from implicitly learning the data manifold rather than the full distribution. Our claim is based on a novel analysis of scores in the small-$\sigma$ regime that reveals a sharp separation of scales: information about the data manifold is $\Theta(\sigma^{-2})$ stronger than information about the distribution. We argue that this insight suggests a paradigm shift from the less practical goal of distributional learning to the more attainable task of geometric learning, which provably tolerates $O(\sigma^{-2})$ larger errors in score approximation. We illustrate this perspective through three consequences: i) in diffusion models, concentration on data support can be achieved with a score error of $o(\sigma^{-2})$, whereas recovering the specific data distribution requires a much stricter $o(1)$ error; ii) more surprisingly, learning the uniform distribution on the manifold-an especially structured and useful object-is also $O(\sigma^{-2})$ easier; and iii) in Bayesian inverse problems, the maximum entropy prior is $O(\sigma^{-2})$ more robust to score errors than generic priors. Finally, we validate our theoretical findings with preliminary experiments on large-scale models, including Stable Diffusion.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a new perspective on score-based generative models, arguing that their success stems from implicitly learning the data manifold geometry rather than fully recovering the data distribution. It demonstrates a sharp separation of scales in the low-noise regime: information about the manifold is Θ(σ⁻²) stronger than density information. This suggests a paradigm shift from distributional learning to geometric learning, which tolerates larger score approximation errors.  The authors illustrate this through concentration on data support with o(σ⁻²) error, improved learning of uniform distributions on manifolds, and enhanced robustness of maximum entropy priors in Bayesian inverse problems. The theoretical findings are validated with experiments, including Stable Diffusion.  The paper advocates for geometric learning as a more practical and robust objective, especially in the low-temperature regime.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of separating geometric learning from distributional learning and highlighting the rate separation is novel. The analysis demonstrating that manifold geometry dominates at a faster rate (Θ(σ⁻²)) than density information (Θ(1)) in the low-noise limit is a significant and insightful contribution. The suggestion to target uniform manifold sampling directly, and the algorithm for achieving this with relaxed score error requirements (o(σ⁻²)), offers a fresh perspective compared to works focused on distributional convergence.  However, the manifold hypothesis itself is not new, and some previous works have implicitly touched upon the idea that these models capture data support well. The key contribution is the explicit separation of scales.

* **Significance:** The paper's insights have important implications for score-based model training and application.
    * It provides a potential explanation for why diffusion models can generate realistic samples without perfectly capturing the data distribution.
    * The proposed algorithm for uniform manifold sampling opens avenues for applications that rely on accurate geometric representations (e.g., manifold learning, exploration, Laplace-Beltrami estimation), and may reduce dataset bias.
    * The theoretical results on Bayesian inverse problems offer valuable guidelines for prior selection and score learning accuracy.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear theoretical analysis:** The mathematical arguments are well-structured and generally convincing.
    * **Practical implications:** The paper provides actionable insights, such as the Tampered Score (TS) Langevin dynamics for improved uniform sampling and a modified PC sampler.
    * **Experimental validation:** The experiments, while preliminary, provide support for the theoretical claims, including demonstrating the advantages of TS Langevin dynamics in a large-scale image generation model.
    * **Well-written and clearly motivated:** The introduction effectively highlights the challenges of score-based learning in the low-noise regime and motivates the need for a new perspective.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Experimental scope:** The experimental validation could be more extensive. More thorough ablation studies and comparisons with other manifold learning techniques would strengthen the empirical evidence.
    * **Assumptions:** The theoretical analysis relies on several assumptions, including the manifold hypothesis and regularity conditions. While these are common in the field, it is important to acknowledge their limitations and consider scenarios where they may not hold.  Specifically the assumption Pdata ∈ C2(U) for Theorem 5.2 is rather strict.
    * **Generality:** The results are primarily focused on the low-noise regime. Extending the analysis to a wider range of noise levels would enhance the paper's applicability.
    * **Limited comparison to alternatives:** While the paper compares to DDPM and PC, additional comparisons to other manifold learning methods and generative techniques that explicitly incorporate geometric priors could further contextualize its contributions.
    * **Connection to existing manifold learning research:** The paper could benefit from a more thorough discussion and comparison to the broader manifold learning literature. While uniform sampling is discussed in the intro, many algorithms have been proposed which explicitly encourage uniform sampling.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in score-based generative modeling by shifting the focus towards geometric learning. The proposed algorithm and theoretical insights could inspire new training strategies and applications that prioritize manifold structure over distributional accuracy. The framework may also have a significant impact on how diffusion models are applied in the inverse problem setting.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant perspective on score-based generative models by emphasizing the importance of geometric learning and the rate separation phenomenon. While the experimental validation could be expanded, and the assumptions need to be carefully considered, the theoretical analysis and practical implications make this a valuable contribution to the field. It offers a solid foundation for future research in this area.

### Segmentor-Guided Counterfactual Fine-Tuning for Image Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24913v1)
- **Authors**: Tian Xia, Matthew Sinclair, Andreas Schuh, Fabio De Sousa Ribeiro, Raghav Mehta, Rajat Rasal, Esther Puyol-Antón, Samuel Gerber, Kersten Petersen, Michiel Schaap, Ben Glocker
- **Abstract**: Counterfactual image generation is a powerful tool for augmenting training data, de-biasing datasets, and modeling disease. Current approaches rely on external classifiers or regressors to increase the effectiveness of subject-level interventions (e.g., changing the patient's age). For structure-specific interventions (e.g., changing the area of the left lung in a chest radiograph), we show that this is insufficient, and can result in undesirable global effects across the image domain. Previous work used pixel-level label maps as guidance, requiring a user to provide hypothetical segmentations which are tedious and difficult to obtain. We propose Segmentor-guided Counterfactual Fine-Tuning (Seg-CFT), which preserves the simplicity of intervening on scalar-valued, structure-specific variables while producing locally coherent and effective counterfactuals. We demonstrate the capability of generating realistic chest radiographs, and we show promising results for modeling coronary artery disease. Code: https://github.com/biomedia-mira/seg-cft.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Segmentor-guided Counterfactual Fine-Tuning (Seg-CFT), a novel method for generating counterfactual images with localized and targeted interventions, specifically in medical imaging. It addresses the limitations of existing counterfactual fine-tuning approaches (Reg-CFT), which often rely on external classifiers or regressors and produce undesirable global effects when applied to structure-specific interventions. Seg-CFT leverages pre-trained, weight-frozen segmentors to guide the counterfactual generation process. The method uses scalar-valued variables (e.g., lung area) as guiding signals but incorporates spatial context through segmentation, leading to more coherent and targeted modifications. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of Seg-CFT on chest radiographs and coronary artery disease modeling, showing improved performance compared to Reg-CFT in generating realistic and spatially consistent counterfactual images.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the incorporation of segmentation maps into the counterfactual fine-tuning process *without* requiring users to manually define counterfactual segmentations. This is achieved by using *pre-trained* (and frozen during DSCM training) segmentors. By using segmentation maps to guide the counterfactual generation, Seg-CFT addresses the limitations of purely regressor-based methods, which can struggle with spatially coherent and localized modifications, leading to spurious correlations. The idea of using frozen segmentors is novel.

**Significance:** The significance stems from several factors:

*   **Improved Counterfactual Realism:** By generating more realistic and localized counterfactual images, Seg-CFT provides a more reliable tool for tasks like data augmentation, bias mitigation, explainability, and disease progression modeling in medical imaging.
*   **Ease of Use:** The method preserves the simplicity of intervening on scalar-valued variables, making it user-friendly compared to approaches requiring pixel-level interventions or manual segmentations.
*   **Potential for Clinical Applications:** The ability to simulate alternative clinical scenarios (e.g., "what if treatment A was used instead of treatment B?") has significant implications for clinical decision-making and personalized medicine. By providing spatially aware counterfactuals. It provides a good balance between targeted control and real world images.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing methods for structure-specific interventions.
*   **Well-Defined Method:** The proposed Seg-CFT method is well-defined and explained.
*   **Solid Experimental Results:** The experiments on chest radiographs and coronary artery disease demonstrate the effectiveness of Seg-CFT compared to Reg-CFT. The quantitative results in Table 1 consistently show improvements, and the visual examples in Figures 2 and 3 provide qualitative evidence of better localization and coherence. The comparative analysis clearly highlights its effectiveness.
*   **Reproducibility:** Code is provided.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Applications:** While the experiments cover chest radiographs and coronary artery disease, further validation across different medical imaging modalities and clinical applications would strengthen the claims.
*   **Independence Assumption of Parents:** The authors acknowledge the simplified assumption of independence between parent variables (e.g., LA, CPA, NCPA). Exploring the causal relationships between these variables could further improve the method.
*   **Reliance on Pre-trained Segmentors:** The performance of Seg-CFT is dependent on the accuracy and robustness of the pre-trained segmentors. The potential limitations of the segmentors are not explored in detail.
*   **No specific mention of limitations and societal impact.**

**Justification for Score:**

Seg-CFT offers a valuable contribution to the field of counterfactual image generation, specifically in medical imaging. Its strengths in improving realism and localization, while maintaining ease of use, are commendable.  While the limitations regarding the reliance on pre-trained segmentors and the current independence assumption of structure-specific variables are a concern and weaken the overall contribution, the method demonstrates notable improvements over existing approaches and has the potential for significant impact in various medical imaging applications. The method offers a novel way of dealing with scalar-valued interventional changes, without resulting in overly manipulated images that can occur when using classifiers/regressors, as the segmentation acts as a natural constraint to guide the generative process.

### Inductive Bias and Spectral Properties of Single-Head Attention in High Dimensions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24914v1)
- **Authors**: Fabrizio Boncoraglio, Vittorio Erba, Emanuele Troiani, Florent Krzakala, Lenka Zdeborová
- **Abstract**: We study empirical risk minimization in a single-head tied-attention layer trained on synthetic high-dimensional sequence tasks, given by the recently introduced attention-indexed model. Using tools from random matrix theory, spin-glass physics, and approximate message passing, we derive sharp asymptotics for training and test errors, locate interpolation and recovery thresholds, and characterize the limiting spectral distribution of the learned weights. Weight decay induces an implicit nuclear-norm regularization, favoring low-rank query and key matrices. Leveraging this, we compare the standard factorized training of query and key matrices with a direct parameterization in which their product is trained element-wise, revealing the inductive bias introduced by the factorized form. Remarkably, the predicted spectral distribution echoes empirical trends reported in large-scale transformers, offering a theoretical perspective consistent with these phenomena.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper presents a theoretical analysis of single-head tied-attention layers trained using empirical risk minimization in high-dimensional settings. The authors derive sharp asymptotic formulas for training and test errors, interpolation and recovery thresholds, and the limiting spectral distribution of the learned weights.  They compare factorized and non-factorized parameterizations of the query and key matrices, revealing the inductive biases induced by factorization, particularly the implicit nuclear-norm regularization stemming from weight decay, which promotes low-rank query and key matrices.  The theoretical predictions are validated by simulations and connect to empirical observations regarding the spectral properties of attention weights in large-scale transformers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in providing a sharp asymptotic analysis of attention layers in a high-dimensional regime.  While prior work has examined attention mechanisms theoretically, this paper makes a more detailed connection to spectral properties observed in large models and provides insights into the implicit regularization effects of weight decay. The direct comparison of factorized vs non-factorized parameterizations is also a significant contribution. However, it extends the work of attention-indexed model, but from bayesian to ERM.

*   **Significance:** The findings contribute to a deeper understanding of the inductive biases inherent in attention mechanisms and how these biases affect generalization.  The connection between weight decay and nuclear-norm regularization provides a theoretical justification for the empirical success of low-rank approximations in attention layers. The theoretical reproduction of spectral trends observed in large transformer models adds weight to the analysis. Also, since spectral analysis becomes ever more important for modern architectures, the work has a practical impact. The paper's theoretical insights may inform the design of more efficient and robust attention mechanisms.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Rigorous mathematical analysis with sharp asymptotic results.
    *   Clear connection between theory and empirical observations.
    *   In-depth comparison of factorized and non-factorized attention.
    *   Insightful explanation of implicit regularization and its impact on rank.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The analysis is limited to single-head tied attention, a simplified setting compared to modern transformer architectures. This simplification is a trade-off for analytical tractability, but it reduces the direct applicability of the results.
    *   The assumption of Gaussian input data is restrictive. While the authors claim universality, this needs further validation, and how far one can go from gaussian distributions should be discussed.
    *   The theoretical framework may not fully capture the complexity of optimization dynamics and the impact of specific hyperparameters, like learning rate schedules, on the learned representations, i.e a non-convex optimizer may fall on spurious local minima.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper will likely stimulate further research on the theoretical properties of attention mechanisms, going beyond the simplified setting of the single-head models.
    *   The results may influence the design of regularization strategies for attention layers.
    *   The theoretical framework could be extended to analyze more complex attention variants, such as multi-head or untied attention.
    *   The analysis of spectral properties can motivate the use of parameter efficient methods in transformers and attention based models

**Justification:**

The paper provides a solid theoretical foundation for understanding the spectral properties of attention weights in high-dimensional scenarios. Although the model is fairly simple, the authors still manage to recover important, real world phenomena of spectral properties of attention weights, as well as explaining important concepts as the role of nuclear-norm regularization.

### MASLegalBench: Benchmarking Multi-Agent Systems in Deductive Legal Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24922v1)
- **Authors**: Huihao Jing, Wenbin Hu, Hongyu Luo, Jianhui Yang, Wei Fan, Haoran Li, Yangqiu Song
- **Abstract**: Multi-agent systems (MAS), leveraging the remarkable capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs), show great potential in addressing complex tasks. In this context, integrating MAS with legal tasks is a crucial step. While previous studies have developed legal benchmarks for LLM agents, none are specifically designed to consider the unique advantages of MAS, such as task decomposition, agent specialization, and flexible training. In fact, the lack of evaluation methods limits the potential of MAS in the legal domain. To address this gap, we propose MASLegalBench, a legal benchmark tailored for MAS and designed with a deductive reasoning approach. Our benchmark uses GDPR as the application scenario, encompassing extensive background knowledge and covering complex reasoning processes that effectively reflect the intricacies of real-world legal situations. Furthermore, we manually design various role-based MAS and conduct extensive experiments using different state-of-the-art LLMs. Our results highlight the strengths, limitations, and potential areas for improvement of existing models and MAS architectures.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can help you with that. Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MASLegalBench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate multi-agent systems (MAS) in the context of deductive legal reasoning.  The benchmark uses the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) as its application scenario, featuring real-world reports and legal questions. The authors manually designed various role-based MAS configurations, assigning agents to tasks like fact identification, rule retrieval, alignment analysis, and common-sense reasoning.  They then conduct extensive experiments using different Large Language Models (LLMs) to highlight the strengths, limitations, and potential improvements of existing models and MAS architectures. The paper's findings suggest that richer contexts provided by MAS can improve performance, and that carefully integrating agents specializing in legal rules and common sense is crucial.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the following:

*   **First MAS-Specific Legal Benchmark:** The authors explicitly state this is the first benchmark designed considering the unique advantages of MAS in the legal field, moving beyond LLM-agent benchmarks focusing on individual performance. This is a key contribution, addressing a clear gap. While other legal benchmarks exist, they don't focus on MAS-specific capabilities like task decomposition and agent specialization.
*   **Decomposition from Real-World Legal Cases:** A strong aspect of the benchmark is its derivation from actual legal cases. The problem decomposition isn't artificially constructed; it emerges from the structure of how legal arguments are presented in practice.
*   **IRAC-Based Design:** The integration of the IRAC (Issue, Rule, Application, Conclusion) method into the MAS design is a sound approach, reflecting established legal reasoning processes. The extension of IRAC to include "Common Sense" adds further refinement.
*  **Manual MAS Design:** The manual design of various MAS is valuable. It creates foundational MAS configurations. However, this also represents a limitation, since it would be hard to generate new MAS configurations automatically.

**Significance:**

*   **Addresses a Critical Gap:** The lack of evaluation methods for MAS in legal reasoning has limited their potential. MASLegalBench directly tackles this, opening doors for further research and development.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The GDPR scenario adds practical significance, as it's a real-world legal domain with significant implications for businesses and individuals.
*   **Insights into LLM Weaknesses:** The experiments reveal specific weaknesses of LLMs in legal reasoning, particularly in areas like common sense and rule interpretation. This helps guide future research toward addressing these limitations within the MAS framework.
*   **Potential for Impact:**  This benchmark, if adopted by the community, has the potential to become a standard for evaluating legal MAS, driving innovation and improvements in the field.
*   **Comprehensive dataset:** The paper presents a comprehensive dataset that includes 950 questions based on expert-authored court cases. This allows researchers to deeply analyse legal reasoning with MAS.
*  **MAS Configuration comparison**: The comparison among MAS configuration represents an important first step. Future research should address the MAS optimization question.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Automation:** The MAS configurations are manually designed. While valuable for initial exploration, it limits scalability and might not capture the full potential of dynamic agent interaction and adaptation. A more automated approach to MAS design and optimization would enhance the benchmark's long-term value.
*   **Dependency on DeepSeek-v3.1:** Although the study tests different LLMs as Meta-LLMs, the use of DeepSeek-v3.1 for sub-task extraction and data construction imposes some limitations. The performance of the benchmark could change if a different LLM is used for its creation.
*   **Lack of Ablation Studies:**  While different agent combinations are tested, a more granular ablation study, isolating the specific contribution of each agent type (AR, CS), would strengthen the conclusions. Also a cost-benefit analysis of adding a new MAS agent may be desirable.
*   **Ethical considerations**: Even though the paper states that it respects the ICLR ethical code, this work potentially impacts legal practices. It is thus necessary to discuss biases in this context.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7.5** to this paper. Here's the reasoning:

*   The paper provides a clearly-defined novel benchmark dataset and MAS configurations for evaluating legal reasoning capabilities. The research addresses a real need in the intersection of MAS and law.
*   The experimental results and derived insights contribute valuable understanding to the community.
*   The work is solid and well-executed. The data collection, analysis, and experimental setups appear rigorous.

However, the limitations discussed above prevent the paper from achieving a higher score. The reliance on manually designed agents and LLM dependency constrains the scalability and generalizability of the findings, slightly reducing its overall impact. Also a formal explainability methodology should be adopted for describing the contribution of each agent.

### When Greedy Wins: Emergent Exploitation Bias in Meta-Bandit LLM Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24923v1)
- **Authors**: Sanxing Chen, Xiaoyin Chen, Yukun Huang, Roy Xie, Bhuwan Dhingra
- **Abstract**: While Large Language Models (LLMs) hold promise to become autonomous agents, they often explore suboptimally in sequential decision-making. Recent work has sought to enhance this capability via supervised fine-tuning (SFT) or reinforcement learning (RL), improving regret on the classic multi-armed bandit task. However, it remains unclear how these learning methods shape exploration strategies and how well they generalize. We investigate both paradigms by training LLMs with SFT on expert trajectories and RL with a range of tailored reward signals including a strategic, regret-shaped reward to reduce variance, and an algorithmic reward that enables oracle imitation. The resulting agents outperform pre-trained models and achieve performance comparable to Upper Confidence Bound (UCB) and Thompson Sampling, with robust generalization to 6x longer horizons and across bandit families. Behavioral analysis reveals that gains often stem from more sophisticated but greedier exploitation: RL/SFT agents are more prone to early catastrophic failure than pre-trained models, prematurely abandoning exploration. Furthermore, agents trained to imitate UCB learn to outperform their teacher by adopting more exploitative variants. Our findings clarify when each training paradigm is preferable and advocate tailored reward design and evaluation beyond average regret to promote robust exploratory behavior.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "When Greedy Wins: Emergent Exploitation Bias in Meta-Bandit LLM Training":

**Summary:**

This paper investigates how Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Reinforcement Learning (RL) shape the exploration-exploitation strategies of Large Language Models (LLMs) in multi-armed bandit (MAB) tasks. The authors train LLMs using SFT on expert trajectories and RL with various reward signals (including regret-shaped and algorithmic rewards to encourage oracle imitation). They find that while both SFT and RL improve LLM performance compared to pre-trained models and achieve results comparable to UCB and Thompson Sampling, gains often stem from increased, and sometimes detrimental, exploitation.  RL/SFT agents are more prone to early catastrophic failures, prematurely abandoning exploration. The paper highlights the importance of tailored reward design and evaluation metrics beyond average regret to promote robust exploration.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Systematic Study:** The paper presents a well-designed and comprehensive study comparing SFT and RL for training LLMs on MAB tasks. The experimental setup is rigorous, with clearly defined reward signals, evaluation environments, and performance metrics.

    *   **Novel Reward Designs:** Introducing strategic (regret-shaped) and algorithmic rewards is a valuable contribution. These novel designs demonstrate how reward shaping can influence LLM exploration behavior and improve training efficiency and generalization.

    *   **Behavioral Analysis:** Going beyond aggregate regret metrics is a major strength. The paper's behavioral analysis, using metrics like suffix failure rate and greedy action frequency, provides insights into the mechanistic differences between the learned policies and reveals potential failure modes associated with premature exploitation.

    *   **Practical Implications:** The findings have practical implications for training LLMs to be more effective decision-makers in sequential tasks, particularly in scenarios where robust exploration is crucial. The study illuminates the trade-offs between optimizing for average performance and ensuring long-term robustness.

    *   **Focus on Algorithmic Reward:** The RL-ALG approach of directly incentivizing agreement with a UCB policy has clear benefits, while highlighting important challenges regarding potential failure modes due to flawed calculations in generated rationales. This approach merits further exploration.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Scale:** While the 3B and 7B models employed are non-trivial, evaluation of the same techniques with larger LLMs (e.g., in the 100B+ range) is needed to confirm these observations scale up.

    *   **MAB Task Simplification:** The MAB problem, while fundamental, is a simplified representation of real-world sequential decision-making. The conclusions might not directly translate to more complex environments with richer state spaces and action spaces. More complex decision making tasks could highlight even more failures or insights into LLMs ability to balance exploration versus exploitation.

    *   **Reliance on CoT demonstrations:** The paper emphasizes Chain-of-Thought prompting, which could introduce specific biases and impact the generality of the results. The choice of CoT methods should be further scrutinized as a point of potential brittleness.

    *   **Teacher Oracle Limitations:** While UCB has theoretical guarantees, it can be myopic in practice. It could be interesting to compare against teachers employing different exploration techniques, which could reveal other failure modes or insights into what exploration strategies LLMs are able to effectively internalize.

    *   **Reproducibility Concerns:** As with many papers dealing with LLMs, obtaining the exact same results might be challenging due to the proprietary nature of some models and the sensitivity of LLM training to hyperparameters and random seeds. This is a general issue but should be considered when assessing the practical impact of the work.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper presents a valuable contribution by systematically comparing SFT and RL for training LLMs on MAB tasks and revealing a tendency towards premature exploitation. While prior work has explored SFT and RL for MAB with LLMs, this paper goes beyond basic performance comparisons to analyze the agents' behaviors and uncover subtle but critical failure modes. The introduction of novel reward designs and the focus on long-term robustness significantly enhances the field's understanding of how to train LLMs for sequential decision-making.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper demonstrates rigorous experimentation, a novel perspective on LLM training for bandit problems, and practical insights into the exploration-exploitation trade-off. It highlights a critical but often overlooked aspect of LLM agents: a tendency towards detrimental exploitation.  The paper's primary value comes from the behavioral analysis, novel reward designs, and clear discussion of the limitations. However, the use of relatively smaller models and the limited complexity of the MAB task prevent a higher score. While there is work to be done to extend the work across model scales, tasks, and exploration techniques, the paper provides a substantial foundation for future research.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### How Well Do LLMs Imitate Human Writing Style?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24930v1)
- **Authors**: Rebira Jemama, Rajesh Kumar
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) can generate fluent text, but their ability to replicate the distinctive style of a specific human author remains unclear. We present a fast, training-free framework for authorship verification and style imitation analysis. The method integrates TF-IDF character n-grams with transformer embeddings and classifies text pairs through empirical distance distributions, eliminating the need for supervised training or threshold tuning. It achieves 97.5\% accuracy on academic essays and 94.5\% in cross-domain evaluation, while reducing training time by 91.8\% and memory usage by 59\% relative to parameter-based baselines. Using this framework, we evaluate five LLMs from three separate families (Llama, Qwen, Mixtral) across four prompting strategies - zero-shot, one-shot, few-shot, and text completion. Results show that the prompting strategy has a more substantial influence on style fidelity than model size: few-shot prompting yields up to 23.5x higher style-matching accuracy than zero-shot, and completion prompting reaches 99.9\% agreement with the original author's style. Crucially, high-fidelity imitation does not imply human-like unpredictability - human essays average a perplexity of 29.5, whereas matched LLM outputs average only 15.2. These findings demonstrate that stylistic fidelity and statistical detectability are separable, establishing a reproducible basis for future work in authorship modeling, detection, and identity-conditioned generation.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "How Well Do LLMs Imitate Human Writing Style?" proposes a training-free framework for evaluating how effectively Large Language Models (LLMs) can mimic the writing style of specific human authors.  The framework combines TF-IDF character n-grams with transformer embeddings to classify text pairs based on empirical distance distributions.  The authors then use this framework to evaluate several LLMs (Llama, Qwen, Mixtral) under different prompting strategies (zero-shot, one-shot, few-shot, and text completion). The results show that prompting strategy significantly impacts style fidelity, with few-shot and completion achieving the best results. Importantly, the paper also demonstrates that high stylistic fidelity doesn't necessarily equate to human-like unpredictability; LLM-generated text, even when mimicking style well, remains statistically more predictable than human writing (lower perplexity). Finally, the authors present a lightweight, training-free authorship verifier.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Framework:** The primary strength is the introduction of a training-free, model-agnostic framework for style imitation analysis. This provides a valuable tool for researchers to compare LLM capabilities in a standardized and reproducible way. The combination of TF-IDF and transformer embeddings is a sensible approach to capture both surface-level and contextual stylistic cues.  The use of empirical distance distributions avoids subjective threshold tuning, enhancing reproducibility.

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper conducts a thorough evaluation of multiple LLMs and prompting strategies. This comparative analysis yields useful insights into which approaches are most effective for style imitation. The investigation of zero-shot, one-shot, few-shot, and completion prompting provides a valuable perspective on the trade-offs involved.

    *   **Important Finding on Detectability:** The finding that stylistic fidelity and statistical detectability are separable is significant. It highlights that even if an LLM can generate text that closely resembles a specific author's style, it can still be identified as machine-generated through statistical analysis (perplexity).  This has important implications for academic integrity and forensic investigations.

    *   **Lightweight Authorship Verifier:** The construction of a fast, lightweight, training-free authorship verifier contributes to the practicality of the work. Reducing training time and memory footprint is crucial for large-scale experimentation.

    *   **Clear and Well-Structured:** The paper is well-written, clearly structured, and provides sufficient detail about the methodology. The inclusion of the code and data (GitHub link) promotes reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Text Types:** The evaluation is primarily focused on academic essays and conversational essays. While this provides a good starting point, it's important to acknowledge that stylistic imitation may vary significantly across different genres (e.g., creative writing, technical documentation, legal documents). Expanding the evaluation to include a wider range of text types would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.

    *   **Perplexity Metric Limitations:**  While perplexity is a useful metric for assessing predictability, it's not a perfect measure of "human-likeness." There might be other statistical or linguistic features that better capture the nuances of human writing style beyond simple predictability. Using a more diverse set of statistical features, including syntax or discourse patterns, might offer a richer picture.

    *   **Prompting Preambles:** The consistent preamble used to prevent LLMs from adding meta-text is a good idea. However, this might affect certain stylistic features an LLM could produce. This aspect should be explored further.

    *   **Reliance on IvyPanda and EssayForum Datasets:** While the datasets are publicly available, using alternative, more commonly used datasets would make comparing this work to others easier.
    *   **Lack of comparison to supervised learning approaches:** While highlighting the benefits of training-free approaches is good, including a comparison to more computationally expensive supervised learning methods for authorship verification is important for understanding the tradeoffs.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper's novelty lies primarily in the combination of a training-free framework, a comprehensive evaluation of LLMs under various prompting strategies, and the demonstration that high stylistic fidelity does not guarantee indistinguishability from human writing. This combination advances the understanding of LLM capabilities and limitations in a practical and reproducible manner. The framework itself represents a useful tool for future research in this area. The findings related to prompting strategy and detectability contribute valuable insights to the fields of authorship verification, AI detection, and responsible AI development.

    However, the core techniques (TF-IDF, transformer embeddings, cosine similarity) are not individually groundbreaking. The significance stems from the thoughtful integration and application of these techniques to a relevant and timely problem. The work is valuable, but not revolutionary.

**Overall Justification:**

The paper provides a valuable contribution to the field by presenting a well-designed framework and conducting a comprehensive evaluation of LLM style imitation capabilities. The key finding that stylistic fidelity does not eliminate statistical detectability is significant and has important implications. However, the limitations in the scope of text types and reliance on potentially limited datasets prevent it from achieving a higher score. Furthermore, the framework's heavy reliance on combining well-known techniques makes it less novel.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Scalable GANs with Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24935v1)
- **Authors**: Sangeek Hyun, MinKyu Lee, Jae-Pil Heo
- **Abstract**: Scalability has driven recent advances in generative modeling, yet its principles remain underexplored for adversarial learning. We investigate the scalability of Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) through two design choices that have proven to be effective in other types of generative models: training in a compact Variational Autoencoder latent space and adopting purely transformer-based generators and discriminators. Training in latent space enables efficient computation while preserving perceptual fidelity, and this efficiency pairs naturally with plain transformers, whose performance scales with computational budget. Building on these choices, we analyze failure modes that emerge when naively scaling GANs. Specifically, we find issues as underutilization of early layers in the generator and optimization instability as the network scales. Accordingly, we provide simple and scale-friendly solutions as lightweight intermediate supervision and width-aware learning-rate adjustment. Our experiments show that GAT, a purely transformer-based and latent-space GANs, can be easily trained reliably across a wide range of capacities (S through XL). Moreover, GAT-XL/2 achieves state-of-the-art single-step, class-conditional generation performance (FID of 2.96) on ImageNet-256 in just 40 epochs, 6x fewer epochs than strong baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Scalable GANs with Transformers" investigates the scalability of Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) by leveraging two principles effective in other generative models: training in a compact Variational Autoencoder (VAE) latent space and employing purely transformer-based generators and discriminators. The authors identify two key challenges when naively scaling GANs: underutilization of early generator layers and optimization instability. They propose simple, scale-friendly solutions: Multi-level Noise-perturbed image Guidance (MNG) for improved layer-wise utilization, and a width-aware learning-rate adjustment for stable training dynamics. The proposed framework, Generative Adversarial Transformers (GAT), demonstrates reliable training across various capacities and achieves state-of-the-art single-step, class-conditional generation performance on ImageNet-256 with significantly fewer training epochs compared to existing baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The combination of VAE latent space training with purely transformer-based GANs is a novel approach. The identification of the problems associated with scaling GANs in this context (early layer underutilization and optimization instability) are insightful. The proposed solutions, MNG and width-aware learning rate adjustment, are simple and effective, contributing practical insights to GAN training. However, it is important to highlight that transformers in GANs have been explored before, even though the exploration of their scalability wasn't really performed, which lowers the novelty component.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial gap in the literature by exploring the scalability of GANs. Demonstrating that GANs can achieve competitive performance on ImageNet-256 with significantly reduced training epochs is a significant contribution. The simplicity of the solutions makes them readily adoptable and potentially impactful for future GAN research and applications.  The demonstrated single-step generation capabilities retain the key strengths of GANs that are less represented in Diffusion based models. The insights regarding discriminator representation learning are also significant. The impact on the adoption of these is limited given the popularity and capabilities of current Diffusion based architectures, limiting its overall impact on the field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined research questions.
    *   Comprehensive experimental validation across various model scales.
    *   Simple and effective solutions that are easy to implement.
    *   Demonstration of state-of-the-art performance on a benchmark dataset.
    *   Thorough analysis of the proposed methods and their impact on GAN training.
    *   Open to potential improvement and adoption, with latent-space training, transformer GANs being interesting research areas.
    *   Insights on balancing the Generator and Discriminator components.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the solutions are effective, they might be considered somewhat incremental. It could be seen as not being revolutionary.
    *   The evaluation is primarily focused on ImageNet-256. Exploring the performance on other datasets and tasks would further strengthen the paper.
    *   The paper acknowledges that GFLOPs are reported for a single forward pass of the Generator. It is important to note that GAN training stability is also determined by the discriminator.
    *   Although performance is state-of-the-art for single step generation, it is important to note the gap with the results that can be obtained with Diffusion based methods with higher NFE values.

**Overall Score and Justification:**

I would assign this paper a score of **7**.

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to scaling GANs, leveraging transformers and latent space training. It identifies and addresses specific challenges in scaling GANs, providing simple yet effective solutions. The experimental results demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on ImageNet-256, showcasing the potential of the proposed framework. It addresses a gap in GAN research that has been dominated recently by Diffusion architectures. However, while the proposed solutions are effective, they are somewhat incremental to the existing body of GAN-transformer work. Also, the GAN community is currently highly influenced and driven by Diffusion based models due to their generative capabilities and training stability, which may impact the real-world adoption of the GAN architecture.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### MobileLLM-R1: Exploring the Limits of Sub-Billion Language Model Reasoners with Open Training Recipes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24945v1)
- **Authors**: Changsheng Zhao, Ernie Chang, Zechun Liu, Chia-Jung Chang, Wei Wen, Chen Lai, Rick Cao, Yuandong Tian, Raghuraman Krishnamoorthi, Yangyang Shi, Vikas Chandra
- **Abstract**: The paradigm shift in large language models (LLMs) from instinctive responses to chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning has fueled two prevailing assumptions: (1) reasoning capabilities only emerge in sufficiently large models, and (2) such capabilities require training on massive datasets. While the first assumption has already been challenged by recent sub-billion-parameter reasoning models such as Qwen3-0.6B and DeepSeek distilled variants, the second remains largely unquestioned. In this work, we revisit the necessity of scaling to extremely large corpora (>10T tokens) for reasoning emergence. By carefully curating and resampling open-source datasets that we identify as beneficial under our designed metrics, we demonstrate that strong reasoning abilities can emerge with far less data. Specifically, we show that only ~2T tokens of high-quality data are sufficient, and pre-training with 4.2T tokens on the dataset resampled from these ~2T tokens, followed by a established post-training procedure, enables the development of MobileLLM-R1, a series of sub-billion-parameter reasoning models that substantially outperform prior models trained on fully open-sourced data. For example, MobileLLM-R1-950M achieves an AIME score of 15.5, compared to just 0.6 for OLMo-2-1.48B and 0.3 for SmolLM-2-1.7B. Remarkably, despite being trained on only 11.7% of the tokens compared to Qwen3's proprietary 36T-token corpus for pretraining, MobileLLM-R1-950M matches or surpasses Qwen3-0.6B across multiple reasoning benchmarks. To facilitate further research in this direction, we have released the complete training recipe, data sources, data mixing ratio, and model checkpoints, together with the key insights obtained throughout this study.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the MobileLLM-R1 paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MobileLLM-R1, a series of sub-billion parameter language models specifically designed for reasoning. The central argument is that strong reasoning capabilities can emerge with significantly less data than previously assumed, challenging the assumption that massive corpora are necessary.  The authors achieve this by carefully curating and resampling open-source datasets, focusing on data quality and targeted knowledge acquisition.  Key components of their approach include: 1) a benchmark-free, self-evolving data optimization method for pre-training, 2) a data-model co-evolution strategy to adapt to model capacity changes during training, and 3) a structured training curriculum including pre-training, mid-training, and supervised fine-tuning. The resulting MobileLLM-R1 models demonstrate competitive performance on reasoning benchmarks compared to larger models trained on much larger datasets, while remaining parameter-efficient and fully open-source.  The authors release the complete training recipe, data sources, and model checkpoints to facilitate further research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in challenging the prevailing assumption regarding the necessity of massive datasets for reasoning emergence in LLMs, specifically within the sub-billion parameter regime.  While techniques like data curation and targeted training are not entirely new, the authors present a systematic approach to these techniques, combining several components in a unique way. The data-model co-evolution strategy during mid-training seems to be a novel element and a key contributor to the results. The benchmark-free data optimization and distillation approach is a reasonable contribution. The complete open-sourcing of the pipeline is an important attribute.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to lower the barrier to entry for developing reasoning-capable LLMs, especially in resource-constrained environments. Showing that high-quality data and careful curation can substitute for sheer scale opens up opportunities for on-device deployment and other applications where model size is critical. Demonstrating strong performance with a fully open-source pipeline is another significant contribution, encouraging further research and reproducibility. The release of the full code and training data is a valuable resource to the community and a huge credit to the authors.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Statement and Motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of resource-intensive LLMs and motivates the need for smaller, efficient reasoning models.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The authors present a well-defined methodology, including specific data curation techniques, training procedures, and evaluation metrics.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides convincing empirical evidence to support its claims, demonstrating competitive performance on several reasoning benchmarks. It also includes well-designed ablation studies to understand the contribution of different components of the training pipeline.
    *   **Open-Source Contribution:** The complete release of code, training data, and model checkpoints is a major strength, promoting reproducibility and further research.
    *   **Analysis and Insights:**  The paper doesn't just present results; it attempts to analyze *why* certain techniques are effective, offering insights into the dynamics of data curation, model-data co-evolution, and the interplay between pre-training, mid-training, and fine-tuning.
    *   The effort invested in dataset curation, and subsequent model training/validation are of clear benefit to the final result.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Individual Techniques:** The techniques used (data curation, knowledge distillation, etc.) are not entirely novel in isolation. The novelty primarily arises from their combination and systematic application.
    *   **Generalizability to Other Architectures/Domains:** While the paper demonstrates success with the MobileLLM architecture, it remains to be seen how well these techniques generalize to other model architectures or different reasoning domains. The analysis of pre-training data is somewhat domain-specific and could be hard to generalize.
    *   **Potential for Overfitting to Specific Benchmarks:** Although benchmark-free data curation is attempted, the use of specific reasoning SFT datasets in the later stages of training could still introduce some bias or overfitting to those benchmarks. It would be interesting to see how the models perform on newly released benchmarks.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

    *   Shifting the focus from sheer scale to data quality and targeted training for reasoning tasks.
    *   Enabling the development of efficient, on-device LLMs for a wider range of applications.
    *   Providing a fully open-source baseline for researchers to build upon and improve.
    *   Providing insights into the importance of each training stage and data type.
    *   Leading to more efficient use of training resources.

Overall, while the paper builds upon existing techniques, it presents a compelling and well-supported argument for the feasibility of developing strong reasoning capabilities in sub-billion parameter LLMs with carefully curated datasets. The complete open-sourcing and the clear discussion of implementation details will lead to significant follow-on work, and the lessons from this paper can inform training pipelines for LLMs in similar settings.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper offers a significant contribution by showing that well designed training recipes can replace sheer volume of data and model scale in the development of reasoning-capable LLMs, and the open access to data and training pipeline are excellent characteristics that justify the score. The paper's strengths outweigh its limitations, making it a noteworthy contribution to the field, particularly in the area of efficient and open-source LLM development. While individual techniques aren't ground-breaking, their novel combination and systematic application in the sub-billion parameter regime, combined with the excellent open access practices, justify a score towards the upper end of the scale.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Intra-request branch orchestration for efficient LLM reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24957v1)
- **Authors**: Weifan Jiang, Rana Shahout, Yilun Du, Michael Mitzenmacher, Minlan Yu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly rely on inference-time reasoning algorithms such as chain-of-thought and multi-branch reasoning to improve accuracy on complex tasks. These methods, however, substantially increase token usage and per-request latency. Prior work has largely focused on reducing token usage, often at the expense of accuracy, while overlooking other latency factors. We present DUCHESS, an LLM serving system that reduces cost and latency without sacrificing accuracy through intra-request branch orchestration guided by predictions. DUCHESS employs a lightweight linear probing model over LLM layer activations to estimate branch correctness, and its orchestration policy decides whether to terminate, duplicate, or continue a branch. When handling multiple requests, DUCHESS further reduces latency by prioritizing easier reasoning tasks when complexity can be estimated from the prompt. Experiments on three reasoning benchmarks show that DUCHESS consistently improves the token-accuracy Pareto frontier, reducing token usage by 42-63% at matched accuracy compared to self-consistency. In serving with vLLM, DUCHESS reduces mean, median, and tail latencies by 57-81%, 58-85%, and 52-84% with First-Come-First-Served scheduling, and achieves additional gains under difficulty-aware scheduling at higher request rates.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents DUCHESS, an LLM serving system that aims to improve the efficiency of inference-time reasoning algorithms like chain-of-thought and multi-branch reasoning. It achieves this by employing intra-request branch orchestration guided by predictions of branch correctness. DUCHESS uses a lightweight linear probing model over LLM layer activations to predict whether a branch is likely to lead to a correct answer. Based on these predictions, DUCHESS can terminate a branch early, duplicate a promising branch, or continue exploring it. At the request pool level, it can prioritize easier reasoning tasks, similar to Shortest Job First (SJF). Experiments on GSM8K, MMLU, and MATH datasets show that DUCHESS reduces token usage and latency without sacrificing accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its integration of branch prediction within a multi-branch reasoning framework to intelligently manage token generation and resource allocation *within* a single request. This is distinct from prior work that primarily focused on cost-accuracy tradeoffs *across* requests or on simplifying single-branch CoT.  The use of lightweight linear probing based on LLM layer activations for branch prediction is also a relatively novel approach, differing from complex orchestration approaches like auxiliary models or reinforcement learning.  However, many components draw from existing work (e.g., probing, SJF scheduling).

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its ability to improve LLM serving efficiency for reasoning tasks without compromising accuracy. Reducing both token usage and latency is crucial for deploying LLMs in interactive and large-scale applications. By effectively managing branches and prioritizing easier tasks, DUCHESS has the potential to make LLM reasoning more scalable and accessible. The reported latency reductions are substantial and could translate to a better user experience and reduced operational costs.  The demonstration that "easier" requests can be identified and prioritized also points to valuable resource management strategies.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined and relatively simple architecture.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on standard benchmarks.
    *   Demonstrated improvement in both token usage and latency.
    *   Lightweight predictor design that avoids introducing significant overhead.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the experiments cover three datasets, further experiments that cover more diverse tasks might be beneficial.
    *   The complexity-aware scheduling relies on knowing request difficulty, which may not always be available in practice. The predictor for request complexity has limited accuracy.
    *   The performance of DUCHESS relies on the accuracy of the branch correctness predictor. Although the predictor is lightweight, its accuracy could be a bottleneck.
    *   The study could have explored the limitations of the prediction approach more deeply. For example, what types of reasoning are harder to predict?

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the design of future LLM serving systems by highlighting the importance of intra-request branch orchestration and by demonstrating the effectiveness of lightweight prediction models for guiding resource allocation. The findings could encourage researchers to explore similar approaches for other reasoning algorithms and tasks. The observation that performance does not suffer (and may improve) by pruning may be valuable to the community. The simple, activation-based prediction mechanism is compelling.

*   **Missing Information and Limitations:**
    * A more thorough analysis of the types of errors made in branch correctness prediction would strengthen the work.
    * Discussion about the generalizability of the learned models is needed. How well does a model trained on one dataset perform on another (even if poorly)?
    * No specific numbers about operational cost savings are discussed.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel and significant approach to LLM serving efficiency for reasoning tasks. It addresses a crucial problem, provides a well-defined solution, and demonstrates its effectiveness through comprehensive experiments. While there are some limitations related to the accuracy of the branch correctness predictor and dependence on request difficulty labels, the overall contribution is valuable. The simple and easy-to-implement method is also notable. Therefore, a score of 7 is warranted.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### SemanticShield: LLM-Powered Audits Expose Shilling Attacks in Recommender Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24961v1)
- **Authors**: Kaihong Li, Huichi Zhou, Bin Ma, Fangjun Huang
- **Abstract**: Recommender systems (RS) are widely used in e-commerce for personalized suggestions, yet their openness makes them susceptible to shilling attacks, where adversaries inject fake behaviors to manipulate recommendations. Most existing defenses emphasize user-side behaviors while overlooking item-side features such as titles and descriptions that can expose malicious intent. To address this gap, we propose a two-stage detection framework that integrates item-side semantics via large language models (LLMs). The first stage pre-screens suspicious users using low-cost behavioral criteria, and the second stage employs LLM-based auditing to evaluate semantic consistency. Furthermore, we enhance the auditing model through reinforcement fine-tuning on a lightweight LLM with carefully designed reward functions, yielding a specialized detector called SemanticShield. Experiments on six representative attack strategies demonstrate the effectiveness of SemanticShield against shilling attacks, and further evaluation on previously unseen attack methods shows its strong generalization capability. Code is available at https://github.com/FrankenstLee/SemanticShield.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "SemanticShield: LLM-Powered Audits Expose Shilling Attacks in Recommender Systems" introduces a novel two-stage framework for detecting shilling attacks.  This framework leverages large language models (LLMs) to analyze item-side semantics and user behavior in recommender systems.  The first stage pre-screens suspicious users based on behavioral heuristics.  The second stage utilizes LLM-based auditing to assess the semantic consistency of user interactions. The auditing model is further enhanced through reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT), resulting in SemanticShield.  The paper demonstrates that SemanticShield outperforms existing methods in detecting various shilling attack strategies, including previously unseen ones, while also preserving recommendation quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in the integration of item-side semantics, analyzed by LLMs, into shilling attack detection.  Most existing methods primarily focus on user-side behaviors.  The use of LLMs for semantic auditing and the application of reinforcement fine-tuning to create a specialized detection model (SemanticShield) are also novel contributions.  The design of reward functions tailored for malicious user detection in the RFT stage appears carefully considered.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a crucial issue in recommender systems: the vulnerability to shilling attacks.  By incorporating item-side semantics, it tackles a previously overlooked aspect of attack detection, potentially improving robustness against sophisticated attacks. The results show that SemanticShield can effectively detect both existing and unseen attack methods, demonstrating strong generalization. The performance gains over existing baselines are significant, especially regarding the false alarm rate (FAR), which is essential for maintaining user experience. The preservation of recommendation quality after applying the defense is also a valuable outcome.

**Strengths:**

*   **Holistic Approach:** The two-stage framework effectively combines behavioral analysis with semantic auditing.
*   **LLM Integration:** Leverages the power of LLMs for understanding and reasoning about item-side features.
*   **Reinforcement Learning Enhancement:** Uses RFT to improve the auditing model's performance and generalization.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** Evaluates the approach against various attacks and datasets, including unseen attacks.
*   **Recommendation Quality Preservation:** Shows that the defense doesn't significantly degrade recommendation quality.
*   **Low False Alarm Rate:** The method keeps FAR low, maintaining user experience.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** LLM-based auditing can be computationally expensive compared to purely behavioral methods. The paper should discuss the practical implications of deploying such a system, including latency and resource requirements. The selection of Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct mitigates this, but further exploration of trade-offs between model size, accuracy, and cost would be beneficial.
*   **Data Dependency:**  Like any LLM-based system, the approach relies on the availability and quality of item-side semantic information (e.g., titles, descriptions). If this information is sparse or misleading, the effectiveness of the approach might be compromised.
*   **Black-box Nature of LLMs:**  The reasoning process within LLMs can be opaque, making it difficult to understand *why* certain users are flagged as malicious. While the authors include clarity and consistency rewards, further investigation into the LLM's decision-making process would enhance trust and interpretability.
*  **Benchmark:** The baseline performance of DGA-MFCA is 0. This baseline is expected to have much better performance based on prior works.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper is likely to influence the field by encouraging researchers to consider item-side semantics in attack detection. It showcases the potential of LLMs for enhancing recommender system security.  The framework and reward function design could serve as a foundation for future research in this area.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7. The paper makes a significant contribution by integrating item-side semantics and LLMs into shilling attack detection. The results are promising, demonstrating improved detection performance and generalization. The use of RFT is well-motivated and effectively enhances the auditing model. The weaknesses related to computational cost, data dependency, and the black-box nature of LLMs are limitations that warrant further investigation. A higher score could have been justified with a more in-depth analysis of computational efficiency, robustness to variations in data quality, and interpretability of the LLM's decisions. The odd benchmark performance is also concerning.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SecInfer: Preventing Prompt Injection via Inference-time Scaling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24967v1)
- **Authors**: Yupei Liu, Yanting Wang, Yuqi Jia, Jinyuan Jia, Neil Zhenqiang Gong
- **Abstract**: Prompt injection attacks pose a pervasive threat to the security of Large Language Models (LLMs). State-of-the-art prevention-based defenses typically rely on fine-tuning an LLM to enhance its security, but they achieve limited effectiveness against strong attacks. In this work, we propose \emph{SecInfer}, a novel defense against prompt injection attacks built on \emph{inference-time scaling}, an emerging paradigm that boosts LLM capability by allocating more compute resources for reasoning during inference. SecInfer consists of two key steps: \emph{system-prompt-guided sampling}, which generates multiple responses for a given input by exploring diverse reasoning paths through a varied set of system prompts, and \emph{target-task-guided aggregation}, which selects the response most likely to accomplish the intended task. Extensive experiments show that, by leveraging additional compute at inference, SecInfer effectively mitigates both existing and adaptive prompt injection attacks, outperforming state-of-the-art defenses as well as existing inference-time scaling approaches.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SecInfer: Preventing Prompt Injection via Inference-time Scaling":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SecInfer, a novel defense mechanism against prompt injection attacks in Large Language Models (LLMs). SecInfer leverages inference-time scaling, allocating more computational resources during inference to enhance security. It consists of two key steps: system-prompt-guided sampling, which generates diverse responses by exploring different reasoning paths with varied system prompts, and target-task-guided aggregation, which selects the response most likely to accomplish the intended task. Through extensive experiments, the authors demonstrate that SecInfer effectively mitigates existing and adaptive prompt injection attacks, outperforming state-of-the-art defenses and other inference-time scaling approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its unique application of inference-time scaling specifically tailored for prompt injection defense. While inference-time scaling itself is not a new concept, the authors present a principled design (system-prompt-guided sampling and target-task-guided aggregation) that effectively addresses the limitations of prior methods when confronted with adversarial prompts. The combination of generating diverse responses using diverse system prompts and then aggregating them based on the target task is a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** Prompt injection attacks represent a significant security threat to LLMs, and SecInfer tackles this threat head-on. By demonstrably outperforming existing defenses and inference-time scaling techniques, the paper makes a significant step towards securing LLM-based applications. The fact that companies are investing in testing LLMs against these attacks underscores the importance of this research area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Principled Design:** SecInfer's two-step approach is well-reasoned and effectively leverages additional compute to improve robustness.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors conduct extensive experiments across various target tasks, injected tasks, LLMs (both open-source and closed-source), and attack types, including adaptive attacks specifically designed to target SecInfer. This thorough evaluation strengthens the paper's claims.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The demonstrated effectiveness in LLM agent settings (InjecAgent and AgentDojo benchmarks) highlights the practical relevance of SecInfer.
    *   **Analyzed Tradeoffs:** The paper recognizes and addresses the computational overhead introduced by SecInfer and explores parallelization strategies to mitigate it.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Effectiveness in Specific Cases:** The authors acknowledge that SecInfer is less effective when target and injected tasks belong to the same type (e.g., both are sentiment analysis).
    *   **Dependency on Judge LLM:** The performance relies on the capabilities of the "judge" LLM. A weaker judge LLM might lead to suboptimal results.
    *   **Potential for Further Optimization:** While the paper explores parallelization, further optimization of the sampling and aggregation steps could be explored to reduce computational cost.
    *   **Not all Adaptive Attacks are Solved**: There may be stronger adaptive attacks that are not investigated in this paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** SecInfer has the potential to influence the development of more robust and secure LLM-based applications. Its approach of leveraging inference-time scaling could inspire further research into defense mechanisms that dynamically allocate resources to mitigate adversarial attacks. The careful crafting of system prompts and the use of a target-task-guided aggregation method also provide valuable insights for future research.

*   **Long-Term Impact**: As the LLM usage increases in the world and reliance on LLM increases, prompt injection attacks will be more and more problematic. The work is critical towards building systems that are robust and safe to prompt injections.

* **Justification:**
The paper introduces a well-designed and empirically supported defense against a critical security threat to LLMs. While the techniques for diverse sampling or semantic clustering/aggregation might be existing, their application and combination with the specific goal of mitigating prompt injection attacks is novel. The thorough evaluation, practical relevance, and potential influence of SecInfer contribute to its significance.
