### On-the-Fly Data Augmentation for Brain Tumor Segmentation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24973v1)
- **Authors**: Ishika Jain, Siri Willems, Steven Latre, Tom De Schepper
- **Abstract**: Robust segmentation across both pre-treatment and post-treatment glioma scans can be helpful for consistent tumor monitoring and treatment planning. BraTS 2025 Task 1 addresses this by challenging models to generalize across varying tumor appearances throughout the treatment timeline. However, training such generalized models requires access to diverse, high-quality annotated data, which is often limited. While data augmentation can alleviate this, storing large volumes of augmented 3D data is computationally expensive. To address these challenges, we propose an on-the-fly augmentation strategy that dynamically inserts synthetic tumors using pretrained generative adversarial networks (GliGANs) during training. We evaluate three nnU-Net-based models and their ensembles: (1) a baseline without external augmentation, (2) a regular on-the-fly augmented model, and (3) a model with customized on-the-fly augmentation. Built upon the nnU-Net framework, our pipeline leverages pretrained GliGAN weights and tumor insertion methods from prior challenge-winning solutions. An ensemble of the three models achieves lesion-wise Dice scores of 0.79 (ET), 0.749 (NETC), 0.872 (RC), 0.825 (SNFH), 0.79 (TC), and 0.88 (WT) on the online BraTS 2025 validation platform. This work ranked first in the BraTS Lighthouse Challenge 2025 Task 1- Adult Glioma Segmentation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces an on-the-fly data augmentation strategy for brain tumor segmentation using pre-trained generative adversarial networks (GliGANs). The aim is to improve segmentation performance, especially in the context of the BraTS 2025 challenge which focuses on generalizing across pre- and post-treatment glioma scans.  Instead of pre-generating and storing augmented data, the approach dynamically inserts synthetic tumors using GliGANs during the training process of an nnU-Net framework.  The authors evaluate a baseline model, a regular on-the-fly augmented model, and a customized on-the-fly augmented model, along with their ensembles. The customized approach includes targeted strategies like altering tumor class ratios and scaling tumor sizes to address class imbalance and improve the detection of small lesions.  The best-performing ensemble achieved competitive results on the BraTS 2025 online validation platform. Post processing including thresholding was used to enhance results. The work ranked first in the BraTS Lighthouse Challenge 2025 Task 1.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of on-the-fly GAN-based augmentation isn't entirely novel, as GANs have been used for data augmentation in medical imaging previously.  However, the authors present a specific implementation adapted to the BraTS challenge, and leverage previously successful GAN models (GliGAN) to do so.  The specific novelty lies in the *customization* of the on-the-fly approach with targeted strategies for tumor class balancing (replacing SNFH with ET and ET with NETC) and scaling tumors for improved small lesion detection. The integration with nnU-Net is also a useful contribution, allowing for direct application of the technique using a standard, well-performing framework. The approach is somewhat incremental, building upon existing techniques.

* **Significance:** The significance of the work lies primarily in the practical demonstration of the on-the-fly augmentation strategy's effectiveness within the competitive BraTS 2025 challenge. The achieved high ranking on the leaderboard provides empirical evidence of the benefit of the method.  The paper offers a practical solution to the data scarcity problem by avoiding pre-computed augmentation.  The findings on targeted augmentation strategies (class balancing and tumor scaling) are valuable. However, the limitations due to time constraints (incomplete model convergence, and suboptimal hyperparameter tuning) prevent a stronger assessment of the method's ultimate potential. Also, the significant contribution is the implementation and experimentation, and less so a radical methodological breakthrough.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Practical Focus:**  Addresses a real-world challenge (data scarcity) in a practical context (BraTS).
    * **Empirical Validation:** Validated the method via a leaderboard-ranked competition, offering tangible evidence of its benefit.
    * **Targeted Augmentation:**  The class-balancing and tumor scaling strategies show a thoughtful approach to addressing specific limitations of the dataset.
    * **Integration with nnU-Net:**  Demonstrates how to integrate on-the-fly augmentation with a popular segmentation framework.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Novelty:** Builds upon existing GAN-based augmentation techniques.
    * **Limited Convergence:**  Models did not fully converge during training, limiting full potential.
    * **Suboptimal Tuning:** The default nnU-Net settings which do not explicitly account for on-the-fly augmentations, thus this could reduce the final result.
    * **Limited Statistical Analysis:**  While paired t-tests are performed, a more comprehensive statistical analysis of the impact of each component of the customized augmentation strategy would be valuable.

**Overall:** The paper presents a worthwhile implementation of on-the-fly GAN-based augmentation with useful targeted strategies. The competitive results demonstrate its potential. However, some weaknesses like limited statistical analysis and convergence should be overcome to increase the impact.

### Double Descent as a Lens for Sample Efficiency in Autoregressive vs. Discrete Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24974v1)
- **Authors**: Ahmad Fraij, Sam Dauncey
- **Abstract**: Data scarcity drives the need for more sample-efficient large language models. In this work, we use the double descent phenomenon to holistically compare the sample efficiency of discrete diffusion and autoregressive models. We show that discrete diffusion models require larger capacity and more training epochs to escape their underparameterized regime and reach the interpolation threshold. In the strongly overparameterized regime, both models exhibit similar behavior, with neither exhibiting a pronounced second descent in test loss across a large range of model sizes. Overall, our results indicate that autoregressive models are more sample-efficient on small-scale datasets, while discrete diffusion models only become competitive when given sufficient capacity and compute.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the sample efficiency of autoregressive (AR) and discrete diffusion models (DDMs) for language modeling, focusing on data-constrained scenarios.  It uses the double descent phenomenon as a lens to compare how these models perform with varying model capacity and training epochs on the small Shakespeare dataset. The key findings are that DDMs require larger capacity and more training epochs to escape the underparameterized regime compared to AR models, suggesting AR models are more sample-efficient on small datasets.  The paper observes no pronounced second descent for either model on the Shakespeare dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The idea of using double descent to compare AR and DDMs for language modeling is somewhat novel, although the connection to Prabhudesai et al. (2025) which compares AR and diffusion models should be acknowledged as being concurrent, though the novelty comes in exploring models in the overparameterized regime and studying the non-monotonic generalization behavior across the interpolation threshold. While double descent has been studied extensively in other contexts, its application to comparing these specific architectures in language modeling and in the context of sample efficiency is a reasonable, albeit not groundbreaking, contribution.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant problem: improving sample efficiency in language models, given the limitations of scaling purely with data. The comparison of AR and DDMs is timely, given the rise of diffusion models. However, the analysis is limited by the use of a single, very small dataset (Tiny Shakespeare).  The conclusions about sample efficiency might not generalize well to larger, more diverse datasets. It's also unclear how the conclusions relate to downstream task performance, the ultimate measure of a language model's utility.

**Strengths:**

*   The paper provides a relatively clear experimental setup.
*   The visualization using double descent curves is helpful for understanding the behavior of the models.
*   The paper highlights the trade-offs between AR and DDM architectures: AR models might be more sample-efficient initially, while diffusion models potentially offer better scalability and robustness.
*   Code availability is commendable, promoting reproducibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Dataset:** The reliance on the Tiny Shakespeare dataset is a major weakness. The dataset is extremely small and highly structured, and results are very unlikely to generalize.
*   **Lack of Downstream Evaluation:** The paper only considers bits-per-token (BPT) as the evaluation metric. It does not assess the models on any downstream tasks (e.g., text generation, question answering), making it difficult to assess the real-world impact of the observed differences in sample efficiency.
*   **Limited Novelty:** While the specific comparison using double descent on AR vs. DDMs is new, the general area of comparing these architectures is not. The connection between better sample efficiency and *better downstream task performance* should be investigated, but is not.
*   **Lack of Theoretical Justification:**  While the paper empirically observes differences, it doesn't provide strong theoretical insights into *why* diffusion models require more capacity to escape the underparameterized regime. This limits the generalizability of the findings.
*   **No Pronounced Second Descent:** The finding of no pronounced second descent is interesting, but it is not explored in depth or explained from a theoretical perspective. The small and somewhat uniform nature of the Shakespeare dataset should have been considered as a potential cause.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a moderately interesting empirical comparison, but its impact is limited by the small dataset, lack of downstream evaluation, and the absence of strong theoretical insights. The use of the double descent framework is a useful way to visualize the differences between the two architectures, but the conclusions are not particularly surprising given the known properties of AR and DDM models.

### SDPose: Exploiting Diffusion Priors for Out-of-Domain and Robust Pose Estimation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24980v1)
- **Authors**: Shuang Liang, Jing He, Chuanmeizhi Wang, Lejun Liao, Guo Zhang, Yingcong Chen, Yuan Yuan
- **Abstract**: Pre-trained diffusion models provide rich multi-scale latent features and are emerging as powerful vision backbones. While recent works such as Marigold~\citep{ke2024repurposing} and Lotus~\citep{he2024lotus} adapt diffusion priors for dense prediction with strong cross-domain generalization, their potential for structured outputs (e.g., human pose estimation) remains underexplored. In this paper, we propose \textbf{SDPose}, a fine-tuning framework built upon Stable Diffusion to fully exploit pre-trained diffusion priors for human pose estimation. First, rather than modifying cross-attention modules or introducing learnable embeddings, we directly predict keypoint heatmaps in the SD U-Net's image latent space to preserve the original generative priors. Second, we map these latent features into keypoint heatmaps through a lightweight convolutional pose head, which avoids disrupting the pre-trained backbone. Finally, to prevent overfitting and enhance out-of-distribution robustness, we incorporate an auxiliary RGB reconstruction branch that preserves domain-transferable generative semantics. To evaluate robustness under domain shift, we further construct \textbf{COCO-OOD}, a style-transferred variant of COCO with preserved annotations. With just one-fifth of the training schedule used by Sapiens on COCO, SDPose attains parity with Sapiens-1B/2B on the COCO validation set and establishes a new state of the art on the cross-domain benchmarks HumanArt and COCO-OOD. Furthermore, we showcase SDPose as a zero-shot pose annotator for downstream controllable generation tasks, including ControlNet-based image synthesis and video generation, where it delivers qualitatively superior pose guidance.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper based on the information provided.

**Summary:**

The paper "SDPose: Exploiting Diffusion Priors for Out-of-Domain and Robust Pose Estimation" introduces a new fine-tuning framework called SDPose for human pose estimation. SDPose leverages the rich latent features of pre-trained Stable Diffusion models to achieve robust pose estimation, particularly in out-of-domain scenarios. The framework includes three key components: (1) preserving the latent space of Stable Diffusion's U-Net, (2) using a lightweight pose decoder head, and (3) incorporating RGB reconstruction regularization.  The authors also construct a new dataset, COCO-OOD, by style-transferring COCO images to evaluate robustness under domain shift. The results show that SDPose achieves comparable performance to state-of-the-art methods on COCO with significantly fewer training epochs and outperforms them on cross-domain benchmarks like HumanArt and COCO-OOD. The paper further demonstrates the use of SDPose as a zero-shot pose annotator for downstream controllable generation tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its specific approach to leveraging pre-trained diffusion models for pose estimation. Existing works in this area focus on modifying the cross-attention mechanism or introducing learnable embeddings. SDPose instead focuses on directly predicting keypoint heatmaps in the latent space, combined with a specific architectural design minimizing disruption to the diffusion model. Constructing COCO-OOD, while not groundbreaking in itself, adds value by providing a benchmark for assessing generalization specifically tailored to style transfer robustness. The x0 prediction design is inspired by prior work like Lotus, however SDPose focuses on extracting the keypoints in the latent space, and combines it with RGB reconstruction regularlization which could be considered novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its demonstration that pre-trained diffusion models can be effectively utilized for structured prediction tasks like pose estimation, offering an efficient and robust alternative to training from scratch or relying on large, task-specific datasets. The substantial improvement in out-of-domain performance compared to existing methods is a significant contribution, highlighting the potential of diffusion priors for generalization. The zero-shot pose annotation capability for controllable generation is another valuable aspect, opening up possibilities for applications in animation, robotics, and other areas. The paper addresses a relevant problem: the lack of robustness in pose estimation when applied to diverse datasets beyond traditional benchmark datasets.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong empirical results on multiple datasets, demonstrating both in-domain and out-of-domain performance.
    *   Ablation studies that clearly show the contributions of each component of the proposed framework.
    *   Introduction of the COCO-OOD dataset, facilitating further research in domain adaptation for pose estimation.
    *   Clear and well-written presentation, making the approach easy to understand.
    *   Computational efficiency and reduced training requirements compared to large models like Sapiens.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the latent space approach is novel, it is unclear whether the gains are truly significant compared to alternatives, without a proper comparison of other finetuning approaches in the latent space of stable diffusion. There are a number of existing methods for finetuning stable diffusion models and the degree to which SDPose beats these out is unclear.
    *   The architectural choices, such as the deconvolution layer and the 1x1 convolutions, could be considered incremental engineering. Though efficient, they lack a clear theoretical justification for their specific configuration.
    *   The focus is primarily on *how* to leverage diffusion models rather than providing deep insights into *why* diffusion priors are beneficial for pose estimation.  A deeper analysis into the nature of diffusion model features and how they are suited for pose estimation would significantly enhance the paper's impact.
    *   Limited exploration of the impact of timestep selection (t=1000), limiting generalizability.

*   **Potential Influence:** SDPose has the potential to influence future research in pose estimation by encouraging the exploration of pre-trained generative models as robust feature extractors. The proposed framework is relatively simple and efficient, making it attractive for adoption in various applications. The introduction of COCO-OOD can serve as a benchmark for evaluating robustness, further driving research in domain adaptation for pose estimation.

**Justification for the Score:**

Given the novelty in its specific latent space fine-tuning strategy, strong empirical results, the contribution of a new benchmark dataset (COCO-OOD), but also some concerns about the incremental nature of certain architectural choices, the relatively shallow exploration of "why" diffusion priors work, and the limited comparison, the overall contribution warrants a score that leans towards above average but isn't exceptional.

### Random Policy Valuation is Enough for LLM Reasoning with Verifiable Rewards
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24981v1)
- **Authors**: Haoran He, Yuxiao Ye, Qingpeng Cai, Chen Hu, Binxing Jiao, Daxin Jiang, Ling Pan
- **Abstract**: RL with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a promising paradigm for improving the reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs). Current methods rely primarily on policy optimization frameworks like PPO and GRPO, which follow generalized policy iteration that alternates between evaluating the current policy's value and improving the policy based on evaluation. While effective, they often suffer from training instability and diversity collapse, requiring complex heuristic tricks and careful tuning. We observe that standard RLVR in math reasoning can be formalized as a specialized finite-horizon Markov Decision Process with deterministic state transitions, tree-structured dynamics, and binary terminal rewards. Though large in scale, the underlying structure is simpler than general-purpose control settings for which popular RL algorithms (e.g., PPO) were developed, suggesting that several sophisticated techniques in existing methods may be reduced or even omitted. Based on this insight, we prove a surprising result: the optimal action can be recovered from the Q-function of a fixed uniformly random policy, thereby bypassing the generalized policy iteration loop and its associated heuristics. We introduce Random Policy Valuation for Diverse Reasoning (ROVER) to translate this principle into a practical and scalable algorithm for LLM math reasoning, a minimalist yet highly effective RL method that samples actions from a softmax over these uniform-policy Q-values. ROVER preserves diversity throughout training, allowing sustained exploration of multiple valid pathways. Across multiple base models and standard math reasoning benchmarks, ROVER demonstrates superior performance in both \textbf{quality} (\textbf{+8.2} on pass@1, \textbf{+16.8} on pass@256) and \textbf{diversity} (\textbf{+17.6\%}), despite its radical simplification compared to strong, complicated existing methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Random Policy Valuation is Enough for LLM Reasoning with Verifiable Rewards":

**Summary:**

The paper challenges the prevailing paradigm of using policy optimization frameworks like PPO and GRPO for improving the reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) through Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR).  The authors demonstrate theoretically and empirically that for math reasoning tasks, which can be formalized as deterministic, tree-structured Markov Decision Processes (MDPs) with binary terminal rewards, the optimal action can be recovered from the Q-function of a *fixed, uniformly random policy*. Based on this insight, they propose Random Policy Valuation for Diverse Reasoning (ROVER), a minimalist RL algorithm that bypasses the iterative policy evaluation-improvement loop of generalized policy iteration. ROVER samples actions from a softmax over these uniform-policy Q-values, preserving diversity throughout training. The experiments show that ROVER achieves superior performance in both quality and diversity compared to stronger existing RL methods across multiple base models and standard math reasoning benchmarks, while being significantly simpler.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the theoretical proof that a uniformly random policy's Q-function contains sufficient information to derive the optimal action in the specific context of math reasoning with deterministic, tree-structured MDPs and binary rewards. This is a surprising result that runs counter to the intuition that uniform policies are inherently uninformative for control. The ROVER algorithm is a direct consequence of this theoretical finding, translating it into a practical and scalable approach for LLM math reasoning. The idea of intrinsic Q-function parameterization based on the LLM parameters without a separate value network also adds value from an implementation point of view.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the dramatic simplification of the RLVR process for LLM reasoning. By eliminating the iterative policy iteration loop and its associated heuristics, ROVER offers a potentially more stable and computationally efficient alternative. The reported empirical results support this claim, showing superior performance in both quality and diversity with a much simpler algorithm. This simplification can lead to more accessible and scalable solutions for enhancing LLM reasoning capabilities. It addresses the common problems of training instability and diversity collapse seen with more complex RLVR algorithms.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong theoretical grounding: The paper provides a clear and rigorous theoretical analysis that justifies the proposed approach. The proof regarding optimal action recovery from a uniform policy's Q-function is a significant contribution.
    *   Radical simplification: ROVER offers a significantly simpler alternative to existing RLVR methods, making it easier to implement and potentially more robust.
    *   Empirical validation: The paper presents comprehensive experimental results across diverse tasks, models, and metrics, consistently demonstrating ROVER's superior performance.
    *   Diversity preservation: ROVER explicitly addresses the issue of diversity collapse, a common problem with existing RLVR methods.
    *   The observation that ROVER can find novel reasoning strategies absent in the base model further indicates the potential for breakthrough gains as a result of leveraging this approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited Scope: The theoretical result is specific to deterministic, tree-structured MDPs with binary rewards, which might limit the applicability of ROVER to other LLM reasoning tasks. Although math reasoning tasks are an important benchmark, this constraint might not be explicitly emphasised throughout the article.
    *   Approximation: The practical implementation of ROVER introduces approximations to handle large action spaces and long horizons, which could impact its performance in certain scenarios.
    *   Lack of Analysis Beyond Performance: While the paper focuses on empirical performance and diversity, it could benefit from a deeper analysis of *why* ROVER works so well, beyond the theoretical result. Further insight into the value landscape learned by the uniform policy would be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** ROVER has the potential to influence the field by:
    *   Shifting the focus from complex policy optimization techniques to simpler, more theoretically grounded approaches for specific LLM reasoning tasks.
    *   Inspiring the development of new RLVR algorithms that exploit the structure of the underlying MDP.
    *   Providing a more accessible and scalable solution for enhancing LLM reasoning capabilities.

*   **Score:** 8

**Justification:** The paper presents a surprising and significant result that challenges the prevailing paradigm for RLVR in LLM reasoning. The theoretical analysis is strong, and the empirical validation is convincing. The radical simplification of the algorithm makes it potentially more robust and accessible. While the scope is limited by the assumptions of deterministic, tree-structured MDPs and binary rewards, the paper offers a valuable contribution that could inspire new directions in the field and enable breakthrough gains in LLM performance.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Path Diffuser: Diffusion Model for Data-Driven Traffic Simulator
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24995v1)
- **Authors**: Da Saem Lee, Akash Karthikeyan, Yash Vardhan Pant, Sebastian Fischmeister
- **Abstract**: Simulating diverse and realistic traffic scenarios is critical for developing and testing autonomous planning. Traditional rule-based planners lack diversity and realism, while learning-based simulators often replay, forecast, or edit scenarios using historical agent trajectories. However, they struggle to generate new scenarios, limiting scalability and diversity due to their reliance on fully annotated logs and historical data. Thus, a key challenge for a learning-based simulator's performance is that it requires agents' past trajectories and pose information in addition to map data, which might not be available for all agents on the road.Without which, generated scenarios often produce unrealistic trajectories that deviate from drivable areas, particularly under out-of-distribution (OOD) map scenes (e.g., curved roads). To address this, we propose Path Diffuser (PD): a two-stage, diffusion model for generating agent pose initializations and their corresponding trajectories conditioned on the map, free of any historical context of agents' trajectories. Furthermore, PD incorporates a motion primitive-based prior, leveraging Frenet frame candidate trajectories to enhance diversity while ensuring road-compliant trajectory generation. We also explore various design choices for modeling complex multi-agent interactions. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method through extensive experiments on the Argoverse2 Dataset and additionally evaluate the generalizability of the approach on OOD map variants. Notably, Path Diffuser outperforms the baseline methods by 1.92x on distribution metrics, 1.14x on common-sense metrics, and 1.62x on road compliance from adversarial benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Path Diffuser: Diffusion Model for Data-Driven Traffic Simulator":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Path Diffuser (PD), a two-stage diffusion model designed to generate realistic and diverse traffic scenarios.  PD addresses the challenge of creating traffic simulations without relying on extensive historical trajectory data, which is often unavailable or incomplete. The first stage initializes agent poses conditioned on map data, using a differential transformer to reduce attention noise and ensure spatial consistency. The second stage generates agent trajectories, conditioned on the initialized poses and map, leveraging Frenet frame candidates to encourage diversity and adherence to road constraints.  The authors demonstrate that PD outperforms baseline methods in terms of distributional realism, common-sense metrics (collision rate, etc.), and robustness to out-of-distribution map variants.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has several novel components. The combination of a two-stage diffusion model with a differential transformer for agent initialization appears novel, particularly the use of a differential transformer to reduce noise in the attention mechanism and to make the agent initialization more sensitive to local spatial context within the map. The integration of Frenet frame candidates as a prior for trajectory generation within a diffusion model is a worthwhile addition that significantly improved performance compared to existing method without priors. The decomposition into initialization and trajectory generation, conditioned only on map data, is also a significant contribution to the realm of traffic simulators, enabling the model to perform trajectory generation without depending on the agent's past.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its ability to generate realistic and diverse traffic scenarios without relying on complete historical trajectory data. This addresses a key limitation of many existing data-driven traffic simulation methods. The ability to handle out-of-distribution (OOD) map variants also enhances the robustness and generalizability of the approach.  The proposed solution also enhances the controllability of traffic generation, potentially leading to improved testing and evaluation of autonomous driving systems. This could lead to more robust and safer autonomous vehicles.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Handles missing data:** The primary strength is the decoupling of agent initialization and trajectory generation, allowing the simulator to function with only map data and without historical trajectory information.
    *   **Robustness:** Demonstrated robustness to OOD map variations.
    *   **Performance:** Demonstrable improvements over existing baseline models in terms of both common-sense metrics and realism.
    *   **Design choices:** Thoughtful design choices regarding the incorporation of the differential transformer to handle spatial context and Frenet Frames as a prior for trajectory generation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **PCA limitation:** Using PCA limits the trajectory horizons and makes it less flexible. While the authors acknowledge this limitation and propose exploring alternative representations as future work, this remains a significant constraint.
    *   **Computational cost:** Training remains computationally intensive (10 hours), although improvements to inference speed are planned.
    *   **Closed-loop evaluation:** The model hasn't been evaluated in a closed-loop setting, which would provide more information about its ability to simulate real world conditions, especially in the presence of accumulative errors.
    *   **Candidate Grid:** The selection of the candidate trajectories is currently a fixed pre-defined grid and might not be robust to more complex cases.

*   **Potential influence:** The influence of this paper could be considerable.  It offers a more scalable and generalizable approach to traffic simulation, which can be used to improve autonomous driving systems, traffic management, and urban planning. The diffusion model's architecture also offers the potential for further refinements and extensions to address more complex scenarios.

*   **Areas for Improvement:**

    *   Exploring alternative latent space representations beyond PCA is crucial to overcome the trajectory horizon limitation.
    *   Evaluating the model in a closed-loop setting would be important to assess long-term stability and error accumulation.
    *   Developing a more flexible method of generating trajectory candidates, rather than relying on the pre-defined grid.
    *   Improvements in both training and inference time would further enable more practical use cases.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** The paper presents a well-designed and effective approach to traffic simulation. The novelty of the two-stage diffusion framework, coupled with the differential transformer and Frenet frame candidates, is significant. The experimental results convincingly demonstrate improvements over existing methods. While limitations exist, such as the PCA-based representation and the computational cost, the work represents a notable advancement in the field and holds significant potential for influencing the development of autonomous driving systems and traffic management. The score reflects this balance of strong contributions and identified limitations with room for improvement in future work.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 8/10

### LVT: Large-Scale Scene Reconstruction via Local View Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25001v1)
- **Authors**: Tooba Imtiaz, Lucy Chai, Kathryn Heal, Xuan Luo, Jungyeon Park, Jennifer Dy, John Flynn
- **Abstract**: Large transformer models are proving to be a powerful tool for 3D vision and novel view synthesis. However, the standard Transformer's well-known quadratic complexity makes it difficult to scale these methods to large scenes. To address this challenge, we propose the Local View Transformer (LVT), a large-scale scene reconstruction and novel view synthesis architecture that circumvents the need for the quadratic attention operation. Motivated by the insight that spatially nearby views provide more useful signal about the local scene composition than distant views, our model processes all information in a local neighborhood around each view. To attend to tokens in nearby views, we leverage a novel positional encoding that conditions on the relative geometric transformation between the query and nearby views. We decode the output of our model into a 3D Gaussian Splat scene representation that includes both color and opacity view-dependence. Taken together, the Local View Transformer enables reconstruction of arbitrarily large, high-resolution scenes in a single forward pass. See our project page for results and interactive demos https://toobaimt.github.io/lvt/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the LVT paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces Local View Transformer (LVT), a novel architecture for large-scale scene reconstruction and novel view synthesis.  LVT addresses the quadratic complexity of standard transformers, making them difficult to scale to large scenes with many high-resolution images. The core idea is to replace global self-attention with local attention, where each view only attends to its spatial neighbors.  This is motivated by the intuition that spatially nearby views provide more useful information about the local scene structure.  To achieve transformation invariance, LVT conditions the attention mechanism on relative poses between views. The model outputs 3D Gaussian splats with view-dependent color and opacity, enabling high-fidelity rendering, especially for thin structures and complex lighting. The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on challenging benchmarks and shows strong generalization capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the **local attention mechanism** applied to novel view synthesis.  While local attention mechanisms have been used in other contexts (e.g., CNNs), its application to transformer-based scene reconstruction, along with the relative pose conditioning, is a novel contribution. The use of view-dependent opacity is also presented as a novel improvement. However, the overall architecture follows a transformer-based pipeline, and the decoding into 3D Gaussians is inspired by GS-LRM.

*   **Significance:** The paper significantly addresses the **scalability limitations** of existing transformer-based methods for NVS. The linear complexity of LVT makes it practical to reconstruct large scenes with high-resolution imagery, something that was previously challenging. The achieved performance and the demonstrated generalization capabilities have great potential to influence the field by paving the way for more practical applications of neural rendering.
    The approach is generally well-written and the experiments appear thoroughly conducted and carefully ablatiated.
    However, the ablation on LVT-Flash is not conclusive, and I would have liked to see more experiments.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a critical bottleneck in the field (scalability).
    *   Introduces a novel and efficient local attention mechanism.
    *   Achieves state-of-the-art results on challenging benchmarks.
    *   Demonstrates strong generalization capabilities.
    *   Comprehensive ablation studies validate design choices.
    *   Well-documented approach and clear presentation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The overall architecture largely follows existing transformer-based pipelines.
    *   View-dependent opacity introduces occasional "popping" artifacts, which require a tradeoff with reduced rendering quality to address.
    *   Focus is mainly on image quality, perhaps some additional discussion regarding efficiency gains could be helpful.
    *   Runtime comparisons against prior work, and detailed runtime analyses would strengthen the paper.
    *   The LVT-Flash ablation does not present strong conclusive evidence, which is a weakness that requires more attention.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has high potential to influence the field by:
    *   Enabling neural rendering for larger and more complex scenes.
    *   Inspiring new research into efficient attention mechanisms for 3D vision.
    *   Providing a practical and scalable solution for novel view synthesis.

**Justification for Score:**

LVT introduces a significant architectural change to NVS by leveraging a more appropriate local view attention mechanism. While LVT builds upon existing architectures, it does provide a good solution to mitigate the quadratic complexity of transformers and achieve state-of-the-art results on large-scale benchmarks with high resolution imagery. Therefore, I assign this paper a high-mid range score with the above considerations in mind.
Score: 7.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Score-based Membership Inference on Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25003v1)
- **Authors**: Mingxing Rao, Bowen Qu, Daniel Moyer
- **Abstract**: Membership inference attacks (MIAs) against diffusion models have emerged as a pressing privacy concern, as these models may inadvertently reveal whether a given sample was part of their training set. We present a theoretical and empirical study of score-based MIAs, focusing on the predicted noise vectors that diffusion models learn to approximate. We show that the expected denoiser output points toward a kernel-weighted local mean of nearby training samples, such that its norm encodes proximity to the training set and thereby reveals membership. Building on this observation, we propose SimA, a single-query attack that provides a principled, efficient alternative to existing multi-query methods. SimA achieves consistently strong performance across variants of DDPM, Latent Diffusion Model (LDM). Notably, we find that Latent Diffusion Models are surprisingly less vulnerable than pixel-space models, due to the strong information bottleneck imposed by their latent auto-encoder. We further investigate this by differing the regularization hyperparameters ($\beta$ in $\beta$-VAE) in latent channel and suggest a strategy to make LDM training more robust to MIA. Our results solidify the theory of score-based MIAs, while highlighting that Latent Diffusion class of methods requires better understanding of inversion for VAE, and not simply inversion of the Diffusion process
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates membership inference attacks (MIAs) against diffusion models, focusing on the predicted noise vectors used during denoising. The authors demonstrate theoretically and empirically that the expected denoiser output points towards a kernel-weighted local mean of training samples, allowing a single-query attack, "SimA," to infer membership based on the norm of the estimated score. Notably, the paper finds that Latent Diffusion Models (LDMs) are surprisingly more robust to these attacks than pixel-space models due to the information bottleneck imposed by their latent auto-encoders. The study also examines the impact of regularization hyperparameters in the latent VAE and suggests strategies for improving LDM robustness to MIAs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its detailed theoretical analysis of score-based MIAs in diffusion models, leading to the formulation of the SimA attack. While other MIA methods exist, SimA presents a more principled and efficient alternative. The observation regarding the increased robustness of LDMs compared to pixel-space models is a significant finding. The investigation into the influence of VAE regularization parameters on LDM vulnerability to MIAs adds further value. While membership inference attacks themselves are not new, the focus on diffusion models and the analysis of LDM vulnerabilities is a distinct contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical privacy concern associated with diffusion models – the potential for unintentional leakage of training data membership. Demonstrating a simple and effective attack (SimA) underscores the vulnerability. The finding about LDM's relative robustness is also highly significant, but even more interesting is the investigation of the VAE component. Revealing how regularization impacts LDM robustness provides valuable insights for developing more secure generative models. This has implications for the deployment of diffusion models in privacy-sensitive applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Rigorous theoretical analysis providing a strong foundation for the proposed attack.
    *   Empirical validation of the attack's effectiveness across different diffusion model variants and datasets.
    *   Identification of the reduced vulnerability of LDMs and an analysis of the underlying reasons related to the latent space representation.
    *   Investigation into the impact of VAE regularization and potential mitigation strategies.
    *   Clearly written and well-structured.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper is primarily focused on the *existence* of the vulnerability. While some mitigation strategies are proposed (tweaking VAE regularization), the effectiveness and generalizability of these strategies are not fully explored.
    *   The theoretical analysis, while rigorous, relies on certain assumptions that might not always hold in practice. The impact of these assumptions on the attack's performance is not explicitly discussed.
    *   The study could benefit from a more extensive exploration of the trade-offs between generative quality and MIA robustness when adjusting VAE parameters.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on MIA defenses for diffusion models. It highlights the importance of considering the entire generative pipeline, including the VAE component in LDMs, rather than solely focusing on the diffusion process itself. The analysis could spur the development of novel regularization techniques or architectural modifications that enhance privacy without sacrificing generative performance.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of 7.  The paper presents a solid contribution with theoretical grounding, empirical support, and novel insights into the vulnerability of diffusion models to MIAs. The investigation into LDMs' robustness and the role of VAE regularization adds substantial value. The weaknesses are primarily related to the limited exploration of comprehensive defense mechanisms and a lack of deeper analysis into the assumptions underlying the theoretical framework. While not a revolutionary breakthrough, this work significantly advances our understanding of privacy risks associated with diffusion models and provides valuable directions for future research. The relatively low performance on Latent Diffusion Models is also another reason to not assign a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### CLPO: Curriculum Learning meets Policy Optimization for LLM Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25004v1)
- **Authors**: Shijie Zhang, Guohao Sun, Kevin Zhang, Xiang Guo, Rujun Guo
- **Abstract**: Recently, online Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has become a key paradigm for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, existing methods typically treat all training samples uniformly, overlooking the vast differences in problem difficulty relative to the model's current capabilities. This uniform training strategy leads to inefficient exploration of problems the model has already mastered, while concurrently lacking effective guidance on problems that are challenging its abilities the most, limiting both learning efficiency and upper-bound performance. To address this, we propose CLPO (Curriculum-guided Learning for Policy Optimization), a novel algorithm that creates a dynamic pedagogical feedback loop within the policy optimization process. The core of CLPO leverages the model's own rollout performance to conduct real-time difficulty assessment, thereby constructing an Online Curriculum. This curriculum then guides an Adaptive Problem Restructuring mechanism, where the model acts as its own teacher: it diversifies medium-difficulty problems to promote generalization and simplifies challenging problems to make them more attainable. Our approach transforms the static training procedure into a dynamic process that co-evolves with the model's capabilities. Experiments show that CLPO achieves state-of-the-art performance across eight challenging mathematical and general reasoning benchmarks, with an average pass@1 improvement of 6.96% over other methods, demonstrating its potential for more efficiently training more capable reasoning models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the CLPO paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CLPO (Curriculum-guided Learning for Policy Optimization), a novel reinforcement learning algorithm designed to enhance the reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs).  CLPO addresses the inefficiency of standard RLVR (Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards) methods that treat all training samples uniformly.  CLPO leverages the model's own rollout performance to dynamically assess problem difficulty, constructing an online curriculum. This curriculum then guides an adaptive problem restructuring mechanism, where the model acts as its own teacher by diversifying medium-difficulty problems and simplifying hard problems.  Finally, CLPO integrates a difficulty-aware policy optimization strategy via dynamic KL regularization. Experiments across various mathematical and general reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that CLPO achieves state-of-the-art performance, outperforming existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novelty:**  The core idea of using the LLM's own performance to create a dynamic curriculum is a strong contribution. This "guided self-evolution" is a potentially powerful concept. The adaptive problem restructuring and difficulty-aware policy optimization are also novel components.
    *   **Significance:** Improving the efficiency of RLVR is significant because it's a promising paradigm for enhancing LLM reasoning. The observed performance gains across a diverse set of benchmarks suggest that CLPO has the potential to make RLVR more practical and effective. The focus on endogenous learning, without reliance on external guidance (expert trajectories or external models), is a major strength.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper presents a thorough experimental evaluation, including comparisons with state-of-the-art methods, ablation studies, and test-time scaling analysis. The use of various datasets including in-domain and OOD benchmarks adds robustness to the experimental findings.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper explains the algorithm clearly and provides detailed implementation details. The pseudocode in the appendix, the explicit prompts, and the figures help to understand the approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** CLPO introduces several components, making it more complex than simpler RLVR algorithms. It is important to check if the individual gains that the framework achieved are due to its combined performance or the influence of a particular component. The ablation studies provide some insight, but further analysis may be helpful.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The performance of curriculum learning methods is often sensitive to hyperparameter settings (e.g., difficulty thresholds, KL scaling factors). While the authors provide specific values, it's not clear how robust the results are to changes in these settings. A more detailed sensitivity analysis or adaptive hyperparameter tuning strategy could strengthen the paper. The impact of hyperparameters like the difficulty range deserves further study.
    *   **Generalizability:** While the paper demonstrates strong results in mathematical and general reasoning, the effectiveness of CLPO in other complex reasoning domains (e.g., code generation, scientific inquiry) remains to be seen. It is important to test the method across domains with different properties.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:**  While the paper provides a high-level theoretical comparison between CLPO and GRPO, it lacks a more formal theoretical analysis of CLPO's convergence properties or sample complexity. Including this would provide a deeper understanding of the algorithm's behavior.
    *   **Limited Insight:** Although the diversification and simplification are important operations, the framework does not detail the properties that medium or hard problems should possess, which limits the application of this solution.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   CLPO has the potential to become a widely used algorithm for LLM reasoning enhancement, especially in domains where verifiable rewards are available.
    *   The "guided self-evolution" paradigm could inspire new approaches to reinforcement learning and curriculum learning.
    *   The focus on endogenous learning could reduce the dependence on costly external data and models.
    *   The framework is likely to be useful to others studying the effect of training data.

**Justification for Score:**

The CLPO paper presents a novel and well-evaluated algorithm with the potential to significantly advance the field of LLM reasoning. The strengths of the paper (novelty, significance, comprehensive evaluation, clarity) outweigh its weaknesses. However, the relative complexity of the algorithm, the uncertainty about the hyperparameter robustness and generalizability, and the limited theoretical understanding prevent it from achieving a higher score. The lack of insight as to what properties of the problems allow them to classify as easy/medium/hard reduces the influence of the solution.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### MARCOS: Deep Thinking by Markov Chain of Continuous Thoughts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25020v1)
- **Authors**: Jiayu Liu, Zhenya Huang, Anya Sims, Enhong Chen, Yee Whye Teh, Ning Miao
- **Abstract**: The current paradigm for reasoning in large language models (LLMs) involves models "thinking out loud" via a sequence of tokens, known as chain-of-thought (CoT). This approach, while effective, has several significant drawbacks. Firstly, inference requires autoregressive generation of often thousands of CoT tokens, which is slow and computationally expensive. Secondly, it constrains reasoning to the discrete space of tokens, creating an information bottleneck across reasoning steps. Thirdly, it fundamentally entangles reasoning with token generation, forcing LLMs to "think while speaking," which causes potentially short-sighted reasoning. In light of these limitations, we re-imagine reasoning in LLMs and present a new paradigm: MARCOS. In our approach, rather than autoregressively generating tokens, we model reasoning as a hidden Markov chain of continuous, high-dimensional "thoughts". Each reasoning step involves a transition of the internal thoughts, where explicit reasoning steps (which may consist of hundreds of tokens) serve as observable variables, which are windows to peek into the implicit thoughts. Since this latent process is incompatible with the standard supervised learning, we further propose a two-phase variational training scheme. Our experiments on three benchmarks demonstrate that MARCOS outperforms existing continuous reasoning methods and, for the first time, achieves performance comparable to token-based CoT, even surpassing it by 4.7% on GSM8K with up to 15.7x speedup in inference. Beyond this, MARCOS offers additional advantages, such as step-level instead of token-level control over randomness, opening significant opportunities for reinforcement learning and reasoning in LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the MARCOS paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MARCOS, a novel paradigm for reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs). Instead of relying on the standard chain-of-thought (CoT) approach which autoregressively generates tokens, MARCOS models reasoning as a hidden Markov chain of continuous "thoughts".  Each reasoning step involves a transition between these continuous, high-dimensional thought vectors.  The discrete CoT tokens are treated as observations of these hidden states. The method incorporates a two-phase variational training scheme to handle the latent process.  Experiments on GSM8K, SVAMP, and MultiArith benchmarks demonstrate that MARCOS outperforms continuous reasoning baselines and achieves performance comparable to, and in some cases surpassing, token-based CoT while significantly improving inference speed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of modeling reasoning as a latent Markov chain of *continuous* thought vectors is a significant departure from the conventional token-based CoT paradigm and previous continuous reasoning methods.  The disentanglement of thinking and speaking into separate modules, with the ability to control randomness at a step-level, distinguishes it further.  The two-phase variational training scheme is also a necessary and novel contribution to enable learning with the latent Markov structure. However, continuous reasoning approaches are not entirely new, but the Markov chain modeling with explicit control of randomness is a novel twist.
* **Significance:** The potential impact of MARCOS is substantial. The ability to accelerate inference while maintaining (or even improving) accuracy addresses a key limitation of current LLM reasoning approaches. The disentanglement of thinking and speaking opens up opportunities for more sophisticated control and steering of the reasoning process, including the possibility of reinforcement learning at the step level rather than at the token level. The demonstrations of step-level randomness control and near-NAR decoding validate the value of this disentanglement. While CoT is known to improve reasoning ability, the fact that MARCOS even surpasses CoT shows that latent thought has value.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** MARCOS demonstrates convincing performance gains across several benchmarks.  The speedup in inference time is particularly noteworthy.
    * **Principled Design:**  The method is well-motivated and grounded in neuroscience (thinking/speaking separation). The design choices, such as the variational training and the separate deep and shallow neuron groups, are clearly explained.
    * **Ablation Studies and Analysis:**  The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of different components. The analysis of how the random variable (Rk) controls different aspects of reasoning is particularly interesting and opens up promising avenues for further research.
    * **Code availability:** Providing the code is an important move for reproducibility.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Training from Scratch:**  The model is trained from scratch, unlike most state-of-the-art LLMs that leverage pre-training on massive datasets. While this allows for a fair comparison with the baselines (also trained from scratch), it is a limitation in terms of absolute performance and potentially generalizability. Pre-training strategies need to be explored in the future. The authors point out potential future directions in the Appendix which shows that they are taking this issue seriously.
    * **Limited Dataset Diversity:** Most experiments focus on mathematical reasoning.  The generalizability to other reasoning tasks (e.g., commonsense reasoning, logical reasoning) should be investigated.
    * **Basic NAR Decoding:** The non-autoregressive decoding experiments are preliminary, and more advanced NAR techniques could potentially yield greater gains.
    * **Reliance on CoT:** It would be more valuable if the continuous reasoning method can be successful without the presence of CoT at all. While the paper acknowledges the value of CoT (mainly as a baseline), this also shows that the current model is relying on that supervision.

* **Potential Influence:** MARCOS could significantly influence the field by shifting the focus from token-based reasoning to continuous, latent representations. This could lead to new architectures and training strategies that enable more efficient, controllable, and ultimately more powerful reasoning in LLMs.  The disentanglement of thinking and speaking, and the step-level control of randomness, could inspire new approaches to reinforcement learning for reasoning. The findings are particularly relevant to scenarios where compute is a bottleneck, and explainable and controllable reasoning is needed.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:** MARCOS presents a novel and well-executed paradigm for LLM reasoning with the potential to significantly impact the field. The strong empirical results, principled design, and insightful analysis justify a high score. The primary limitations are the training from scratch, the focus on mathematical reasoning, and the early-stage NAR decoding experiments. However, these limitations also represent opportunities for future research and development. While continuous reasoning methods are not entirely new, MARCOS provides a novel approach to controlling randomness with the Markov Chain representation.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### STAGE: Stable and Generalizable GRPO for Autoregressive Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25027v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoxiao Ma, Haibo Qiu, Guohui Zhang, Zhixiong Zeng, Siqi Yang, Lin Ma, Feng Zhao
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning has recently been explored to improve text-to-image generation, yet applying existing GRPO algorithms to autoregressive (AR) image models remains challenging. The instability of the training process easily disrupts the pretrained model capability during long runs, resulting in marginal gains, degraded image quality, and poor generalization. In this work, we revisit GRPO for AR image generation and identify two key issues: contradictory gradients from unnecessary tokens and unstable policy entropy dynamics. To address these, we introduce STAGE, a stable and generalizable framework that leverages two targeted solutions: 1) Advantage/KL reweighting. Similarity-aware reweighting to alleviate conflicting updates; and 2) Entropy reward. An entropy-based reward corresponding to reference model to stabilize learning. With the help of alleviating conflicts between tokens and an entropy reward for stabilizing training, we reduce disruption of the pretrained distribution and mitigate reward hacking, which in turn improves generalization and transfer better to other benchmarks. Experiments across multiple benchmarks show that STAGE consistently improves visual quality, stability, and cross-task generalization compared to baseline GRPO.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "STAGE: Stable and Generalizable GRPO for Autoregressive Image Generation" addresses the challenges of applying Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to autoregressive (AR) image generation models. It identifies two key issues: conflicting gradients arising from unnecessary tokens and unstable policy entropy dynamics. To combat these, the paper introduces STAGE, a framework that incorporates similarity-aware advantage/KL reweighting to mitigate conflicting updates and an entropy-based reward to stabilize learning. The authors demonstrate that STAGE improves visual quality, stability, and cross-task generalization across various benchmarks compared to baseline GRPO implementations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper identifies a genuine problem in applying GRPO to AR image generation, which is a valuable contribution. The proposed solutions, similarity-aware reweighting, and entropy reward, are sound approaches to address the identified issues. However, neither of these techniques is entirely groundbreaking on its own. Similarity-based methods are already used to address conflicting gradients in other learning scenarios, and entropy regularization is a well-known technique for stabilizing RL. The novelty lies in the specific adaptation and combination of these techniques within the context of AR image generation using GRPO. The similarity-aware reweighting in the image context, contrasting with text-based methods, is a definite element of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper has a definite significance within the field of image generation using RL. Existing GRPO implementations for AR image generation are relatively unexplored, and the paper provides insights into their limitations. The proposed STAGE framework offers a practical and effective solution that improves stability and generalization, which are crucial for real-world applications. The performance improvements demonstrated on various benchmarks are noteworthy and suggest that STAGE can potentially advance the state-of-the-art in AR image generation using GRPO. The work’s extensive ablation studies offer concrete data for practitioners and researchers.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clearly identifies key challenges in applying GRPO to AR image generation.
    *   Proposes a well-defined framework (STAGE) with targeted solutions.
    *   Demonstrates empirical improvements in visual quality, stability, and generalization on multiple benchmarks.
    *   Provides ablation studies to evaluate the contribution of each component.
    *   Code availability fosters reproducibility and further research.
    *   Well-written and clearly explained methodology.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The individual techniques (similarity reweighting and entropy reward) are not entirely new.
    *   The hyperparameter tuning and sensitivity to reward functions could be better discussed. While ablation studies provide insight, a more thorough investigation could highlight the robustness (or lack thereof) of the approach.
    *   The diversity limitations of AR generation are mentioned but could be more thoroughly addressed.
    *   While the evaluation is comprehensive, most metrics are based on prompt adherence and structural consistency. A greater focus on human evaluation would further strengthen the findings.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in this area by highlighting the importance of stability and generalization in GRPO for AR image generation. It provides a practical and effective solution that can be used as a foundation for further improvements. The paper can also encourage researchers to explore new techniques for token selection and weighting to improve image quality and diversity.

*   **Critical Considerations:** The paper's focus on stability and generalization is highly pertinent, given the current trend towards scaling up image generation models. The demonstrated benefits of STAGE are likely to become increasingly important as AR models become more complex and are applied to more diverse tasks. However, it is crucial to carefully consider the limitations of the approach, particularly regarding diversity, and to develop new techniques that can further improve the performance and robustness of AR image generation using GRPO.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by identifying and addressing key challenges in applying GRPO to AR image generation. While the individual techniques may not be entirely groundbreaking, their adaptation and combination within STAGE offers a practical and effective solution that significantly improves performance. The paper's strengths, including its thorough evaluation and clear presentation, outweigh its weaknesses, making it a noteworthy contribution that is likely to influence future research in this area. The limited attention to potential diversity decreases as well as somewhat standard RL methodology justifies limiting this to an upper level of significance within the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### VT-FSL: Bridging Vision and Text with LLMs for Few-Shot Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25033v1)
- **Authors**: Wenhao Li, Qiangchang Wang, Xianjing Meng, Zhibin Wu, Yilong Yin
- **Abstract**: Few-shot learning (FSL) aims to recognize novel concepts from only a few labeled support samples. Recent studies enhance support features by incorporating additional semantic information or designing complex semantic fusion modules. However, they still suffer from hallucinating semantics that contradict the visual evidence due to the lack of grounding in actual instances, resulting in noisy guidance and costly corrections. To address these issues, we propose a novel framework, bridging Vision and Text with LLMs for Few-Shot Learning (VT-FSL), which constructs precise cross-modal prompts conditioned on Large Language Models (LLMs) and support images, seamlessly integrating them through a geometry-aware alignment. It mainly consists of Cross-modal Iterative Prompting (CIP) and Cross-modal Geometric Alignment (CGA). Specifically, the CIP conditions an LLM on both class names and support images to generate precise class descriptions iteratively in a single structured reasoning pass. These descriptions not only enrich the semantic understanding of novel classes but also enable the zero-shot synthesis of semantically consistent images. The descriptions and synthetic images act respectively as complementary textual and visual prompts, providing high-level class semantics and low-level intra-class diversity to compensate for limited support data. Furthermore, the CGA jointly aligns the fused textual, support, and synthetic visual representations by minimizing the kernelized volume of the 3-dimensional parallelotope they span. It captures global and nonlinear relationships among all representations, enabling structured and consistent multimodal integration. The proposed VT-FSL method establishes new state-of-the-art performance across ten diverse benchmarks, including standard, cross-domain, and fine-grained few-shot learning scenarios. Code is available at https://github.com/peacelwh/VT-FSL.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "VT-FSL: Bridging Vision and Text with LLMs for Few-Shot Learning":

**Summary:**

This paper proposes VT-FSL, a novel framework that aims to improve few-shot learning (FSL) by leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to bridge vision and text modalities. The key idea is to generate precise cross-modal prompts conditioned on LLMs and support images, then seamlessly integrate them through a geometry-aware alignment mechanism. The framework consists of two main components: (1) Cross-modal Iterative Prompting (CIP), which conditions the LLM on class names and support images to generate refined class descriptions, enabling zero-shot synthesis of semantically consistent images. (2) Cross-modal Geometric Alignment (CGA), which jointly aligns the fused textual, support, and synthetic visual representations by minimizing the kernelized volume of the 3-dimensional parallelotope they span.  Experiments across diverse FSL benchmarks demonstrate that VT-FSL achieves state-of-the-art performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has a few novel components, which merit careful examination. The Cross-modal Iterative Prompting (CIP) is novel because it combines visual information (support images) with class names to condition the LLM. This is a step beyond methods relying solely on class names or external knowledge bases.  The Cross-modal Geometric Alignment (CGA) is also a genuinely new idea. Using the kernelized volume of a parallelotope as a way to align different modalities and capture non-linear relationships is innovative and potentially powerful. The combination of CIP and CGA to achieve FSL is another important novel aspect.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this paper lies in its ability to improve the performance of few-shot learning, a critical area of research in machine learning. Overcoming the limitations of FSL has practical implications in real-world scenarios with limited labeled data. This is particularly true in cases where collecting large datasets is expensive or infeasible.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art results across a wide range of datasets, including standard, cross-domain, and fine-grained FSL benchmarks. The significant improvements in accuracy (4.2% average) are compelling.
    *   **Well-designed Framework:** The VT-FSL framework is logically structured and well-motivated. The CIP and CGA modules are designed to address specific limitations of previous approaches.
    *   **Thorough Ablation Studies:** The paper provides comprehensive ablation studies to analyze the contribution of each component of the framework. This helps to understand the effectiveness of the proposed techniques.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **LLM Dependency:** The method relies heavily on the performance of LLMs and text-to-image models. While the authors claim robustness across different LLMs, the potential for semantic hallucinations and biases in the generated text and images remains a concern. The reliance on LLMs means the approach is not entirely "data-driven".
    *   **Complexity:** The framework is relatively complex, involving multiple stages and modules. This can make it difficult to implement and tune.
    *   **Geometric Interpretation:** While the geometric interpretation of CGA is intriguing, the paper could provide more intuitive explanations for *why* minimizing the kernelized volume leads to better alignment. Deeper insights are needed.
    *   **Computational Cost**: While the authors mention a low inference time, the overall computational cost of the method, including pre-training the LLM and text-to-image model, may be high.

*   **Potential Influence:** The VT-FSL framework has the potential to influence future research in FSL. The use of LLMs for generating informative prompts and the geometric alignment mechanism are promising directions for improving generalization in low-data regimes. However, the practical adoption of the framework will depend on its accessibility and computational cost.

*The score is based on the following criteria:*
*   *Are the claims well supported by the theoretical and/or empirical results?*
        - The empirical results are strong and the ablation studies support the claims about each module's contribution.
*   *Are the results significant in the area of the problem?*
        - The increase in performance in few-shot learning is valuable and addresses limitations in current work.
*   *Is the work clearly presented?*
        - The paper is well-organized and easy to understand.
*   *Are the methods novel?*
       - Yes, the use of volume-based similarity and iterative prompting is novel.
*   *Are the baselines reasonable?*
        - Yes, the paper compares against reasonable baselines.

**Score: 7.5**

*Rationale:* While VT-FSL represents a significant advancement in few-shot learning by effectively integrating LLMs and geometric alignment, the reliance on LLMs and the complexity of the framework limit its broader impact. The novelty of the proposed techniques and the strong empirical results justify a high score, but the weaknesses related to complexity and LLM dependency prevent it from reaching an exceptional rating. The reliance on these models mean that the approach has the potential to inherit issues like semantic hallucinations and bias.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### A multiscale analysis of mean-field transformers in the moderate interaction regime
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25040v1)
- **Authors**: Giuseppe Bruno, Federico Pasqualotto, Andrea Agazzi
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we study the evolution of tokens through the depth of encoder-only transformer models at inference time by modeling them as a system of particles interacting in a mean-field way and studying the corresponding dynamics. More specifically, we consider this problem in the moderate interaction regime, where the number $N$ of tokens is large and the inverse temperature parameter $\beta$ of the model scales together with $N$. In this regime, the dynamics of the system displays a multiscale behavior: a fast phase, where the token empirical measure collapses on a low-dimensional space, an intermediate phase, where the measure further collapses into clusters, and a slow one, where such clusters sequentially merge into a single one. We provide a rigorous characterization of the limiting dynamics in each of these phases and prove convergence in the above mentioned limit, exemplifying our results with some simulations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a multi-scale analysis of the token dynamics in encoder-only transformer models at inference time. The authors model the tokens as a mean-field interacting particle system and investigate its behavior in the *moderate interaction regime*, where the inverse temperature parameter scales linearly with the number of tokens. They identify three distinct dynamical phases: an initial *alignment phase* where tokens quickly collapse onto a low-dimensional subspace, an intermediate *heat phase* (diffusive or anti-diffusive), and a slow *pairing phase* where clusters sequentially merge. The paper characterizes the limiting dynamics in each phase using different mathematical techniques, including transport equations, heat equations, and systems of ODEs. The study aims to provide a theoretical understanding of how internal representations are formed and refined in transformers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The identification of the *alignment phase* as a distinct, fast dynamical process is a significant contribution. While previous works have examined clustering and diffusion-like behavior in transformers, the analysis of the initial collapse onto a lower-dimensional subspace driven by a linear transport equation seems to be genuinely new.  The reconciliation of various previously observed dynamical regimes within a unified multi-timescale framework is also valuable.
*   **Significance:** By studying the moderate interaction regime (β scales with N), the authors tackle a scenario motivated by current scaling strategies in state-of-the-art LLMs. This makes the theoretical insights potentially relevant to practical model design.  The work builds on existing mean-field models, offering a more complete and nuanced picture of token dynamics. A better understanding of this dynamical regime could help guide choices on hyperparameter tuning and the architecture of such models.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The mathematical analysis is rigorous and employs a range of techniques (e.g., Dobrushin-type stability estimates, Laplace approximation).
    *   The paper connects the theoretical findings to empirical observations in transformers, such as rank collapse.
    *   The multi-scale approach provides a coherent framework for understanding different dynamical regimes.
    *   Assumptions are relatively weaker than in previous studies, increasing the applicability of the results.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The results for the heat and pairing phases rely on stronger assumptions compared to the alignment phase. Specifically, assumptions on parameter matrices (e.g. Assumption 4) can limit generality. The requirement of a positive definite matrix may exclude relevant realistic scenarios.
    *   A complete, fully rigorous mathematical treatment, especially for the backward heat equation phase and cluster merging, remains an open challenge. The analysis of the slow clustering dynamics is still somewhat limited, only showing that a limited number of clusters remains static until they interact in a sequential process.
    *   The connection from the moderate temperature and context scaling to real-world performance on datasets might be more direct.

*   **Potential Impact:**  The paper's insights could influence future theoretical research on transformers.  The identification of distinct dynamical phases and their mathematical characterization provides a foundation for further investigation of representation learning in these models. This increased understanding of token dynamics will hopefully guide choices on hyperparameter tuning and the architecture of such models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by identifying and characterizing the alignment phase, a previously overlooked aspect of transformer dynamics. The reconciliation of different dynamical regimes into a unified framework is also valuable. However, the stronger assumptions required for later phases and the remaining open challenges in rigorously treating the full dynamics prevent a higher score. I give the score as 7 because it advances our knowledge of transformer dynamics, is relatively mathematically robust, and has potential practical implications for hyperparameter tuning and architecture development.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10
