### GRACE-MoE: Grouping and Replication with Locality-Aware Routing for Efficient Distributed MoE Inference
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25041v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Han, Lehan Pan, Jie Peng, Ziyang Tao, Wuyang Zhang, Yanyong Zhang
- **Abstract**: Sparse Mixture of Experts (SMoE) performs conditional computation by selectively activating a subset of experts, thereby enabling scalable parameter growth in large language models (LLMs). However, the expanded parameter scale exceeds the memory capacity of a single device, necessitating distributed deployment for inference. This setup introduces two critical challenges: (1) Communication Issue: Transferring features to devices with activated experts leads to significant communication overhead. (2) Computational Load Issue: Skewed expert activation overloads certain GPUs, resulting in load imbalance across devices. Among these, communication overhead is identified as the main bottleneck in SMoE inference. Nevertheless, reducing communication between devices may exacerbate computational load imbalance, leading to device idleness and resource waste. Therefore, we present GRACE-MoE, short for Grouping and Replication with Locality-Aware Routing for SMoE inference. GRACE-MoE is a co-optimization framework that jointly reduces communication overhead and alleviates computational load imbalance. Specifically, the framework comprises two key phases: (1) Grouping & Replication: This phase groups experts based on their affinity to reduce cross-device communication. Additionally, dynamic replication is applied to address load skew, improving computational load balance across GPUs. (2) Routing: This phase employs a locality-aware routing strategy with load prediction. It prioritizes local replicas to minimize communication overhead and balances requests across remote replicas when necessary. Experiments on diverse models and multi-node, multi-GPU environments demonstrate that GRACE-MoE efficiently reduces end-to-end inference latency, achieving up to 3.79x speedup over state-of-the-art systems. Code for GRACE-MoE will be released upon acceptance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "GRACE-MoE: Grouping and Replication with Locality-Aware Routing for Efficient Distributed MoE Inference" addresses the challenges of communication overhead and computational load imbalance in distributed Sparse Mixture of Experts (SMoE) inference.  The paper proposes GRACE-MoE, a framework that consists of two phases: (1) expert grouping and dynamic replication in an offline phase, where experts are grouped based on affinity, and hot experts are replicated according to load skew. (2) a locality-aware routing strategy in an online phase, which prioritizes local replicas and balances requests across remote replicas using a weighted round-robin approach with load prediction. Experiments on various models and multi-node setups demonstrate reduced end-to-end inference latency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper combines several ideas to address the problem of efficient distributed MoE inference. While affinity-based grouping and dynamic replication have been explored before (e.g., C2R), the integration of these techniques with locality-aware routing, specifically targeted at multi-node setups, shows some novelty. The approach to dynamically replicate experts based on load skew within *the heaviest group only* seems like a useful strategy to mitigate load imbalance without excessive memory overhead. The topology-aware routing and load prediction also add to the novelty. However, the individual components are not revolutionary, and the value comes primarily from the combination and tuning for this specific problem.

*   **Significance:**  Distributed MoE inference is a critical area for scaling large language models. Reducing inference latency and improving resource utilization directly impact the practicality of deploying these models. The improvements demonstrated by GRACE-MoE (up to 3.79x speedup) are substantial and demonstrate the potential impact of the work. The study tackles a crucial challenge that existing methods only partially address, especially in cross-node scenarios. The component analysis section provides valuable insights into the contribution of each piece of the pipeline.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive approach:** The paper provides a holistic framework that considers both communication and computational load, unlike many existing works that focus only on one aspect.
    *   **Practical application:** The method directly addresses the challenges of large-scale MoE deployment in multi-node environments, making it relevant to real-world applications.
    *   **Clear evaluation:** The paper presents extensive experimental results with multiple models and setups, thoroughly comparing to existing baselines. Component analysis is also a strong point.
    *   **Well-written and organized:** The paper is easy to follow and the motivation and methodology are clearly presented.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental improvement:** While effective, the techniques are largely based on existing ideas.  The paper does not introduce any fundamentally new algorithm or theoretical insight.
    *   **Limited datasets/models:** Evaluation, although detailed, could benefit from more diverse models. Also, WikiText is a commonly used dataset but isn't the most demanding in terms of computational load/communication which makes the generalisability harder to judge.
    *   **Expert selection/replication: ** How much does the performance depend on good "profiling"? The paper doesn't explore the consequences of a low-quality profiling phase. A simple experiment investigating a "perturbed" profiling phase would strengthen the analysis.
    *   **Memory consumption:** The discussion of memory overhead (related to replication) is minimal. A more detailed analysis of memory footprint would be useful.
    *   **Tuning complexity:** The paper introduces hyperparameters related to group size deviation. Tuning such parameters is important to consider in practical deployments.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in distributed MoE inference. The results clearly demonstrate the benefits of joint optimization of communication and load balance, encouraging similar approaches. The specific techniques developed could be adopted or adapted by other researchers and practitioners.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of distributed MoE inference by providing a comprehensive framework that effectively reduces latency and improves resource utilization in multi-node environments. While the individual components are not entirely novel, the combination of affinity-based grouping, dynamic replication, and locality-aware routing is well-motivated and yields significant performance gains. However, the incremental nature of the contributions and the limited exploration of profiling quality somewhat diminish the overall impact.

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Models for Software Testing: A Research Roadmap
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25043v1)
- **Authors**: Cristian Augusto, Antonia Bertolino, Guglielmo De Angelis, Francesca Lonetti, Jesús Morán
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are starting to be profiled as one of the most significant disruptions in the Software Testing field. Specifically, they have been successfully applied in software testing tasks such as generating test code, or summarizing documentation. This potential has attracted hundreds of researchers, resulting in dozens of new contributions every month, hardening researchers to stay at the forefront of the wave. Still, to the best of our knowledge, no prior work has provided a structured vision of the progress and most relevant research trends in LLM-based testing. In this article, we aim to provide a roadmap that illustrates its current state, grouping the contributions into different categories, and also sketching the most promising and active research directions for the field. To achieve this objective, we have conducted a semi-systematic literature review, collecting articles and mapping them into the most prominent categories, reviewing the current and ongoing status, and analyzing the open challenges of LLM-based software testing. Lastly, we have outlined several expected long-term impacts of LLMs over the whole software testing field.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and evaluation of the paper, keeping in mind the need for rigorous critique:

**Summary**

The paper "Large Language Models for Software Testing: A Research Roadmap" provides a structured overview of the emerging field of LLM-based software testing.  It aims to map the current state of research, categorize existing contributions, identify open challenges, and forecast the long-term impact of LLMs on software testing.  The authors conduct a semi-systematic literature review (SSLR), classifying papers into categories like Unit Test Generation, High-Level Test Generation, Oracle Generation, Test Augmentation, Non-Functional Testing, and Test Agents. The paper discusses the different LLM approaches (Pure Prompting, Hybrid Prompting, etc.), benchmarks used, and metrics applied in each category. It further explores research challenges related to model configuration, fine-tuning, data leakage, prompt engineering, context management, hallucinations, and evaluation metrics. The roadmap concludes by outlining potential impacts of LLMs on software testing dreams (efficacy, automation, modeling, theory).

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a reasonable attempt to offer a structured view of a rapidly evolving area. Mapping the existing LLM for software testing and categorizing contributions provides some value to researchers navigating this domain.  However, the novelty is somewhat limited due to the semi-systematic approach. This contrasts with full systematic literature review processes, which is justified by the authors.
    The paper does not introduce significantly new technical insights or methodologies but acts more as an organized synthesis of existing trends. This aspect has a substantial role to play in the scoring.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems primarily from its potential to guide researchers. By categorizing existing works, identifying key challenges, and suggesting future directions, it can help researchers focus their efforts on the most promising areas. The roadmap concept itself is valuable, serving as a reference point for understanding the current landscape. However, because this space changes so fast, there might be concerns of the paper being out-of-date, quickly. The authors could have thought about a strategy for continuous maintenance of the roadmap.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Structured Overview:** The paper provides a well-structured categorization of LLM-based testing research, which is helpful for understanding the field's scope.
    *   **Identification of Challenges:** The paper identifies and discusses key challenges related to LLM adoption in software testing, such as data leakage, prompt engineering, and the need for standardized benchmarks.
    *   **Roadmap Concept:** The roadmap approach is useful for guiding future research and highlighting areas that require further investigation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Semi-Systematic Approach:** The SSLR approach raises concerns about completeness and potential bias in the selection of papers. While the authors provide a rationale, it may have omitted some relevant contributions.
    *   **Lack of Technical Depth:**  The paper focuses more on categorization and high-level discussion rather than delving into the technical details of specific LLM-based testing techniques.  This limits its usefulness for researchers seeking in-depth understanding. It presents itself as a roadmap for directions that were known a year ago, and misses the opportunity to set more bold and visionary future directions.
    *   **Rapidly Evolving Field:** As acknowledged by the authors, the field of LLM-based testing is evolving very rapidly. This means that the paper's overview may become outdated relatively quickly.
    *   **Limited Critical Analysis:** While the paper identifies challenges, the critical analysis of existing approaches could be more in-depth.  For example, a more nuanced discussion of the limitations of current LLM models for specific testing tasks would strengthen the analysis. The analysis of the tools that can be found is descriptive and too simplistic, and does not provide relevant and valuable recommendations.
    *   **Lack of Bold Vision:** While the paper forecasts impacts based on existing ideas, it does not provide a bold and visionary perspective on the future of software testing driven by LLMs. It mainly reports about impacts based on trends known one year ago.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's potential influence lies in its ability to provide a starting point and reference for researchers entering the field. By organizing existing research and highlighting key challenges, it can accelerate progress and encourage further investigation.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the paper's strengths and weaknesses, a score of 6 is appropriate.

*   The paper provides a useful and timely overview of a rapidly evolving field (a positive).
*   However, the semi-systematic methodology limits the completeness and potentially introduces bias.
*   The lack of technical depth and limited critical analysis detract from its value for experienced researchers.
*   The rapid pace of change in the field means that the paper may become outdated relatively quickly.

Overall, the paper serves as a helpful guide for navigating the LLM-based software testing landscape but falls short of being a groundbreaking or highly influential contribution.

**Score: 6**
- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Hyperdimensional Probe: Decoding LLM Representations via Vector Symbolic Architectures
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25045v1)
- **Authors**: Marco Bronzini, Carlo Nicolini, Bruno Lepri, Jacopo Staiano, Andrea Passerini
- **Abstract**: Despite their capabilities, Large Language Models (LLMs) remain opaque with limited understanding of their internal representations. Current interpretability methods, such as direct logit attribution (DLA) and sparse autoencoders (SAEs), provide restricted insight due to limitations such as the model's output vocabulary or unclear feature names. This work introduces Hyperdimensional Probe, a novel paradigm for decoding information from the LLM vector space. It combines ideas from symbolic representations and neural probing to project the model's residual stream into interpretable concepts via Vector Symbolic Architectures (VSAs). This probe combines the strengths of SAEs and conventional probes while overcoming their key limitations. We validate our decoding paradigm with controlled input-completion tasks, probing the model's final state before next-token prediction on inputs spanning syntactic pattern recognition, key-value associations, and abstract inference. We further assess it in a question-answering setting, examining the state of the model both before and after text generation. Our experiments show that our probe reliably extracts meaningful concepts across varied LLMs, embedding sizes, and input domains, also helping identify LLM failures. Our work advances information decoding in LLM vector space, enabling extracting more informative, interpretable, and structured features from neural representations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Hyperdimensional Probe," a novel method for interpreting the internal representations of Large Language Models (LLMs). It combines ideas from symbolic representations using Vector Symbolic Architectures (VSAs) and neural probing.  The approach trains a shallow neural network to map the LLM's residual stream into a controlled vector space structured by VSA encodings.  This projection allows for extracting interpretable concepts using hypervector algebra, enabling a deeper understanding of how the LLM encodes information. The paper validates the method on controlled input-completion tasks, examining syntactic pattern recognition, key-value associations, and abstract inference, as well as in a question-answering setting. The experiments demonstrate the method's ability to reliably extract meaningful concepts across varied LLMs and input domains, also assisting in the identification of LLM failures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *integration* of VSA with neural probing in the context of LLM interpretability. While VSAs themselves are not new, their application to decoding LLM residual streams, particularly in conjunction with a trained encoder, is a significant contribution. The approach effectively bridges the gap between connectionist and symbolic representations. Comparing to other methods, it addresses limitations with:
    *   **Supervised Probes**: it alleviates the need to separate information decoding from probe learning.
    *   **DLA**: does not depend on the limitations of the model’s vocabulary, allowing higher level of feature abstraction.
    *   **SAEs**: It offers explicit, pre-defined features names compared to potentially data-dependent and vague features found from SAEs.
*   **Significance:** The work addresses a crucial challenge in the LLM field: the black-box nature of these models.  By offering a more structured and interpretable view of LLM representations, the method has the potential to:
    *   Improve our understanding of how LLMs encode and process information.
    *   Facilitate the debugging of LLM failures.
    *   Enable the development of more reliable and trustworthy AI systems.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper provides a well-defined and explained methodology, outlining the VSA encoding process, neural encoder training, and concept extraction steps.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The method is rigorously validated on a variety of tasks, including controlled input completion and a question-answering task.
    *   **Comparative Analysis:**  The paper provides a comparative analysis against DLA, highlighting the strengths of the VSA-based approach.
    *   **Practical Considerations:** The authors address computational cost by including dimensionality reduction strategies.
    *   **Open-Source Availability:** The code and data release enhance reproducibility and encourages further research.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on a Predefined Vocabulary:** The method depends on a predefined set of concepts to create the VSA encodings. Although the paper mentions no practical limitations on cardinality or types of symbols, the selection of these concepts could influence the results and may require domain-specific knowledge.
    *   **Limited Scope of Input Types:** The primary experiments are conducted on textual analogies, which are relatively structured. While question-answering is explored and there is discussion on generality, more exploration of other input types and tasks would strengthen the claims of broad applicability.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of LLM interpretability. By demonstrating the effectiveness of VSA-based probing, it opens up new avenues for understanding and debugging these powerful models. The method's ability to extract meaningful concepts could also be leveraged in downstream applications, such as toxicity detection or bias mitigation.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a solid and well-validated contribution to the field of LLM interpretability. It introduces a novel and technically sound approach that tackles key limitations of existing methods, whilst also thoroughly testing and evaluating its approach across multiple models. The results are compelling and provide significant evidence of the method's effectiveness. While the method relies on a predefined vocabulary and lacks extensive exploration of diverse input types, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Scaling Synthetic Task Generation for Agents via Exploration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25047v1)
- **Authors**: Ram Ramrakhya, Andrew Szot, Omar Attia, Yuhao Yang, Anh Nguyen, Bogdan Mazoure, Zhe Gan, Harsh Agrawal, Alexander Toshev
- **Abstract**: Post-Training Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to build interactive agents holds promise across domains such as computer-use, web navigation, and robotics. A key challenge in scaling such post-training is lack of high-quality downstream agentic task datasets with tasks that are diverse, feasible, and verifiable. Existing approaches for task generation rely heavily on human annotation or prompting MLLM with limited downstream environment information, which is either costly or poorly scalable as it yield tasks with limited coverage. To remedy this, we present AutoPlay, a scalable pipeline for task generation that explicitly explores interactive environments to discover possible interactions and current state information to synthesize environment-grounded tasks. AutoPlay operates in two stages: (i) an exploration phase, where an MLLM explorer agent systematically uncovers novel environment states and functionalities, and (ii) a task generation phase, where a task generator leverages exploration trajectories and a set of task guideline prompts as context to synthesize diverse, executable, and verifiable tasks. We show AutoPlay generates 20k tasks across 20 Android applications and 10k tasks across 13 applications Ubuntu applications to train mobile-use and computer-use agents. AutoPlay generated tasks enable large-scale task demonstration synthesis without human annotation by employing an MLLM task executor and verifier. This data enables training MLLM-based UI agents that improve success rates up to $20.0\%$ on mobile-use and $10.9\%$ on computer-use scenarios. In addition, AutoPlay generated tasks combined with MLLM verifier-based rewards enable scaling reinforcement learning training of UI agents, leading to an additional $5.7\%$ gain. coverage. These results establish AutoPlay as a scalable approach for post-training capable MLLM agents reducing reliance on human annotation.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Scaling Synthetic Task Generation for Agents via Exploration":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AutoPlay, a novel pipeline for scalable synthetic task generation for training interactive agents, particularly MLLMs. AutoPlay addresses the limitations of existing task generation methods that rely on costly human annotation or lack environment grounding. It operates in two stages: (1) Exploration, where an MLLM agent systematically explores an environment to uncover states and functionalities; and (2) Task Generation, where a task generator leverages exploration trajectories and guideline prompts to synthesize diverse, executable, and verifiable tasks. The authors demonstrate AutoPlay's effectiveness by generating large datasets of UI tasks for Android and Ubuntu platforms. These tasks are used for supervised finetuning and reinforcement learning, resulting in significant performance improvements in trained UI agents.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic exploration-based approach to environment grounding for synthetic task generation.  Existing methods often rely on pre-existing knowledge or limited interaction. AutoPlay's active exploration, memory module, and task guideline prompts create a more diverse and grounded set of tasks. While related techniques (e.g., zero-shot task generation, reinforcement learning with external verifiers) exist, AutoPlay combines these in a novel way specifically for scaling interactive agent training.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a crucial bottleneck in training interactive agents: the lack of high-quality, diverse task datasets.  By automating the task generation process, AutoPlay has the potential to significantly reduce reliance on human annotation and accelerate the development of capable agents across various domains. The demonstrated performance improvements, especially the ability to train agents with both SFT and RL, highlight its practical impact. The experiments, though focused on UI agents, are compelling and clearly presented. The detailed ablation studies further strengthen the argument for the effectiveness of their approach.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The exploration-based approach is innovative and leads to better coverage.
    *   The two-stage pipeline (exploration, task generation) is well-designed and effective.
    *   The experimental results are strong and demonstrate significant performance gains.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of different components.
    *   The paper is clearly written and well-structured.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The evaluation is mainly focused on UI agents. While this is a relevant and practical domain, further demonstration across other domains (e.g., robotics, gaming) would strengthen the generalizability of AutoPlay.
    *   The exploration strategy could be further optimized with more sophisticated exploration algorithms. The current approach relies on a relatively simple exploration strategy for discovering new environment states.
    *   The approach heavily relies on powerful MLLMs (e.g. GPT-4o) for exploration, task generation, execution and verification, raising potential scalability challenges if less powerful models need to be used.
    *   The reliance on MLLMs introduces a dependence on their biases and limitations. Synthetic data can perpetuate existing biases present in the models used to generate it.

*   **Potential Influence:** AutoPlay has the potential to influence the way interactive agents are trained by providing a scalable and automated approach to task generation. It could encourage the development of more sophisticated exploration and task generation techniques, and may be adapted for other interactive domains beyond UI agents. The work could also spur research into improving the quality and diversity of synthetic data for agent training.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of interactive agent training. While the evaluation is primarily focused on UI agents and the approach relies on powerful MLLMs, AutoPlay addresses a critical bottleneck and offers a promising solution. It opens up new avenues for scaling the training of interactive agents, reducing reliance on human annotation. The strong experimental results and detailed ablation studies further support the value of the proposed method.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Confidence-Guided Error Correction for Disordered Speech Recognition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25048v1)
- **Authors**: Abner Hernandez, Tomás Arias Vergara, Andreas Maier, Paula Andrea Pérez-Toro
- **Abstract**: We investigate the use of large language models (LLMs) as post-processing modules for automatic speech recognition (ASR), focusing on their ability to perform error correction for disordered speech. In particular, we propose confidence-informed prompting, where word-level uncertainty estimates are embedded directly into LLM training to improve robustness and generalization across speakers and datasets. This approach directs the model to uncertain ASR regions and reduces overcorrection. We fine-tune a LLaMA 3.1 model and compare our approach to both transcript-only fine-tuning and post hoc confidence-based filtering. Evaluations show that our method achieves a 10% relative WER reduction compared to naive LLM correction on the Speech Accessibility Project spontaneous speech and a 47% reduction on TORGO, demonstrating the effectiveness of confidence-aware fine-tuning for impaired speech.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of automatic speech recognition (ASR) error correction, specifically for disordered speech (dysarthria).  It proposes a confidence-guided framework using large language models (LLMs) to improve ASR accuracy.  The core idea is to fine-tune a LLaMA model, incorporating word-level confidence scores into the input prompts.  This guides the LLM to focus its correction efforts on the uncertain regions of the ASR transcript, mitigating overcorrection. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach on the Speech Accessibility Project (SAP) dataset and TORGO database, showing improved word error rate (WER) compared to naive LLM correction and traditional confidence-based filtering.  The results show generalization across datasets and ASR architectures (Parakeet and Whisper).

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The novelty of this paper lies primarily in the specific application of confidence-guided prompting within an LLM fine-tuning framework for *disordered* speech correction. While the general idea of using LLMs for ASR error correction and confidence scores for guiding correction isn't entirely new (cited papers [5,6,10,11] show prior work), the combination of these elements, specifically tailored for dysarthric speech, adds a valuable contribution.  The careful design of the prompt to explicitly incorporate confidence scores is also noteworthy.  However, the individual components are well-established, which somewhat limits the overall novelty.

* **Significance:** The significance is moderate. Improving ASR for disordered speech is a crucial application with real-world impact for individuals with speech impairments.  The paper's demonstration of improved WER and reduced overcorrection compared to baseline LLM approaches validates the potential of the proposed framework.  The generalization results across different datasets (TORGO and SAP) and ASR architectures (Parakeet and Whisper) strengthens the significance, suggesting that the approach is robust and applicable in diverse scenarios. However, the performance gains, while present, are not groundbreaking, and more extensive experiments with significantly more diverse ASR models and speakers are desirable.  The focus on Phase 1 SAP data, which may be relatively structured commands in nature, might not fully capture the challenges of truly spontaneous dysarthric speech. Furthermore, Phase 2 of the Speech Accessibility Project involves a much more diverse dataset, and an assessment of its generalizability should involve such a diverse setting.

* **Strengths:**
    * Clear problem definition and motivation.
    * Well-designed experiments and comparisons.
    * Demonstration of generalization across datasets and architectures.
    * Qualitative examples effectively illustrating the overcorrection issue and the benefits of the confidence-guided approach.
    * Analysis of helpful and harmful correction rates.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * Relatively small performance gains (especially on TORGO) compared to a large naive model.
    * Limited diversity in the impairment types studied (mostly focused on Parkinson's Disease, although the TORGO dataset contains cerebral palsy and ALS patients.)
    * The paper relies on phase 1 SAP data, which are relatively structured, thereby limiting the difficulty.
    * Lack of comparative evaluation against state-of-the-art ASR systems for dysarthric speech (e.g., adaptations and fine-tuning on existing ASR models, rather than just using out-of-the-box Parakeet and Whisper).
    * The method requires access to the ASR model's internals to get confidence scores, potentially limiting its applicability when only the ASR transcript is available.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper could influence future research by highlighting the importance of confidence-aware error correction for disordered speech. It offers a practical and relatively simple method that can be implemented on top of existing LLM-based ASR systems. Furthermore, the analysis of overcorrection contributes to a better understanding of the challenges involved in applying LLMs to this specific domain.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the moderate novelty, demonstrable but not transformative performance improvements, and limitations in the diversity of speaker and data types (structured vs spontaneous speech and speech impairment type), a score of 6 is appropriate. The work is valuable, addresses a significant problem, and shows promise, but does not represent a major breakthrough. Future work should focus on expanding the diversity of the data, improving the confidence measures, and comparing it against more robust dysarthric speech ASR pipelines.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Advantage Weighted Matching: Aligning RL with Pretraining in Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25050v1)
- **Authors**: Shuchen Xue, Chongjian Ge, Shilong Zhang, Yichen Li, Zhi-Ming Ma
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement Learning (RL) has emerged as a central paradigm for advancing Large Language Models (LLMs), where pre-training and RL post-training share the same log-likelihood formulation. In contrast, recent RL approaches for diffusion models, most notably Denoising Diffusion Policy Optimization (DDPO), optimize an objective different from the pretraining objectives--score/flow matching loss. In this work, we establish a novel theoretical analysis: DDPO is an implicit form of score/flow matching with noisy targets, which increases variance and slows convergence. Building on this analysis, we introduce \textbf{Advantage Weighted Matching (AWM)}, a policy-gradient method for diffusion. It uses the same score/flow-matching loss as pretraining to obtain a lower-variance objective and reweights each sample by its advantage. In effect, AWM raises the influence of high-reward samples and suppresses low-reward ones while keeping the modeling objective identical to pretraining. This unifies pretraining and RL conceptually and practically, is consistent with policy-gradient theory, reduces variance, and yields faster convergence. This simple yet effective design yields substantial benefits: on GenEval, OCR, and PickScore benchmarks, AWM delivers up to a $24\times$ speedup over Flow-GRPO (which builds on DDPO), when applied to Stable Diffusion 3.5 Medium and FLUX, without compromising generation quality. Code is available at https://github.com/scxue/advantage_weighted_matching.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Advantage Weighted Matching: Aligning RL with Pretraining in Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the discrepancy between the objectives used in pretraining and reinforcement learning (RL) fine-tuning of diffusion models.  Pretraining typically uses score/flow matching, while recent RL methods like DDPO optimize a per-step Gaussian likelihood derived from a reverse-time Markov Decision Process (MDP). The authors demonstrate that DDPO implicitly performs score matching with *noisy* data, leading to increased variance and slower convergence. They propose Advantage Weighted Matching (AWM), a method that directly incorporates reward signals into the original score/flow matching objective by reweighting samples based on their advantage. This maintains consistency with pretraining, reduces variance, and improves convergence speed.  Experiments on GenEval, OCR, and PickScore benchmarks show AWM achieves significant speedups over DDPO-based methods without sacrificing generation quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its analysis of DDPO as an implicit form of noisy score matching and the resulting variance issues. While reward-weighted regression isn't entirely new, AWM distinguishes itself by 1) explicitly connecting DDPO to noisy score matching, 2) providing theoretical justification for variance reduction, and 3) demonstrating substantial practical benefits through a simple and well-motivated approach. The derivation of the noisy DSM equivalence and the corresponding theoretical analyses, including the variance quantification, are novel contributions.
*   **Significance:** The significance comes from addressing a key misalignment between pretraining and RL in diffusion models. By demonstrating the equivalence between a standard and widely used RL algorithm (DDPO) with a theoretically suboptimal learning schema (score matching with noisy data), the work provides a powerful argument and solution for streamlining future RL fine-tuning algorithms for diffusion models. AWM offers a practical and effective way to align RL with pretraining, potentially accelerating the development and deployment of high-quality generative models. The consistent improvements across diverse tasks and models support the general applicability of the approach. The speedup results are compelling, suggesting AWM can reduce the compute cost associated with RL fine-tuning.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong theoretical analysis connecting DDPO to noisy score matching.
    *   Clear explanation of variance reduction and its impact on convergence.
    *   Simple and elegant AWM implementation.
    *   Comprehensive experimental validation across diverse tasks and models.
    *   Significant speedups compared to existing methods.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The theoretical analysis focuses primarily on DDPO and might not generalize to all RL-based diffusion methods.
    *   While AWM shows strong performance, the paper could benefit from exploring its robustness to different hyperparameter settings, particularly concerning the KL regularization term. More analysis on when and why uniform weights can be more performant than ELBO-based weights would also be beneficial.
    *   Limited exploration of different samplers and timestep schedules beyond Euler-Maruyama.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in RL for diffusion models by:
    *   Guiding the design of new RL algorithms that are more aligned with pretraining objectives.
    *   Reducing the computational burden of RL fine-tuning, making it more accessible.
    *   Encouraging the adoption of score/flow matching as a unified framework for both pretraining and RL.

**Rationale for Score:**

Given the novelty of the noisy DSM equivalence and its theoretical analysis, the simplicity and effectiveness of the AWM solution, the thorough experimental validation, and the potential for impact on the field, a high score is warranted. However, the limited scope of the theoretical analysis to DDPO and some unexplored parameter choices keep the score from being a 9 or 10.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### CharGen: Fast and Fluent Portrait Modification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25058v1)
- **Authors**: Jan-Niklas Dihlmann, Arnela Killguss, Hendrik P. A. Lensch
- **Abstract**: Interactive editing of character images with diffusion models remains challenging due to the inherent trade-off between fine-grained control, generation speed, and visual fidelity. We introduce CharGen, a character-focused editor that combines attribute-specific Concept Sliders, trained to isolate and manipulate attributes such as facial feature size, expression, and decoration with the StreamDiffusion sampling pipeline for more interactive performance. To counteract the loss of detail that often accompanies accelerated sampling, we propose a lightweight Repair Step that reinstates fine textures without compromising structural consistency. Throughout extensive ablation studies and in comparison to open-source InstructPix2Pix and closed-source Google Gemini, and a comprehensive user study, CharGen achieves two-to-four-fold faster edit turnaround with precise editing control and identity-consistent results. Project page: https://chargen.jdihlmann.com/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CharGen: Fast and Fluent Portrait Modification":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CharGen, a system for interactive and continuous editing of character portraits using diffusion models. CharGen combines attribute-specific Concept Sliders (LoRA adapters trained to control specific facial attributes) with StreamDiffusion (a fast sampling pipeline).  To mitigate detail loss caused by StreamDiffusion's acceleration, a lightweight Repair Step is implemented to restore high-frequency detail without compromising structural consistency. The system achieves faster edit turnaround times than competing methods like InstructPix2Pix and Google Gemini while maintaining precise editing control and identity consistency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of existing techniques in a way that addresses a critical trade-off in interactive generative editing: speed vs. control and quality.  The Concept Sliders are not inherently new, but the system demonstrates a practical and well-engineered integration of these sliders with a faster sampling method. The lightweight Repair Step is also a valuable contribution to offset the loss of fidelity that can occur with accelerated generation. A similar approach to accelerated sampling might be found elsewhere.

* **Significance:** Interactive portrait editing is a valuable and widely used functionality. A system that provides fast and continuous control over various attributes is a significant advancement, especially for tasks like character design and content creation.  The quantitative and qualitative evaluations are convincing, showing that CharGen can effectively balance speed and quality better than existing approaches. The user study is also a strength, providing evidence that users find the system useful and preferable to existing methods.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Addressing a Core Problem:**  The paper effectively tackles the critical trade-off between speed, control, and quality in interactive generative editing.
    * **Well-Integrated System:** The combination of Concept Sliders, StreamDiffusion, and the Repair Step is thoughtfully designed and implemented.
    * **Strong Evaluation:** The quantitative and qualitative evaluations are comprehensive, including comparisons with state-of-the-art methods and a user study.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand, with clear explanations of the methods and experiments.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Novelty of Individual Components:**  While the combination is novel, the individual components (Concept Sliders, StreamDiffusion) are not groundbreaking.
    * **Dependence on Pre-trained Components:** The system relies on pre-trained diffusion models and requires training of Concept Sliders, which may limit its applicability to other domains without significant effort.
    * **Ethical Concerns:** It is stated that CharGen raises ethical concerns but not elaborated upon.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper is likely to have a significant impact on the field of interactive generative editing.  It demonstrates a practical approach to achieving faster editing speeds without sacrificing control or quality.  The system could be integrated into existing character design tools and content creation pipelines.  The work also points to future research directions, such as mitigating interactions between Concept Sliders and improving training methodologies for discrete attributes.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper provides a good solution to an existing issue in a well known field of Computer Graphics. Given that individual components (Concept Sliders, StreamDiffusion) are not groundbreaking, and the lack of discussion about ethical concerns, a score of 7 seems appropriate. It is more than just a clever combination of existing ideas, but demonstrates a well-engineered system that provides practical benefits and guides future research.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Learning from Convenience Samples: A Case Study on Fine-Tuning LLMs for Survey Non-response in the German Longitudinal Election Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25063v1)
- **Authors**: Tobias Holtdirk, Dennis Assenmacher, Arnim Bleier, Claudia Wagner
- **Abstract**: Survey researchers face two key challenges: the rising costs of probability samples and missing data (e.g., non-response or attrition), which can undermine inference and increase the use of convenience samples. Recent work explores using large language models (LLMs) to simulate respondents via persona-based prompts, often without labeled data. We study a more practical setting where partial survey responses exist: we fine-tune LLMs on available data to impute self-reported vote choice under both random and systematic nonresponse, using the German Longitudinal Election Study. We compare zero-shot prompting and supervised fine-tuning against tabular classifiers (e.g., CatBoost) and test how different convenience samples (e.g., students) used for fine-tuning affect generalization. Our results show that when data are missing completely at random, fine-tuned LLMs match tabular classifiers but outperform zero-shot approaches. When only biased convenience samples are available, fine-tuning small (3B to 8B) open-source LLMs can recover both individual-level predictions and population-level distributions more accurately than zero-shot and often better than tabular methods. This suggests fine-tuned LLMs offer a promising strategy for researchers working with non-probability samples or systematic missingness, and may enable new survey designs requiring only easily accessible subpopulations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of fine-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs) for imputing missing vote choice data in surveys, specifically focusing on the German Longitudinal Election Study (GLES).  It contrasts this approach with zero-shot prompting and traditional tabular classifiers (CatBoost, Logistic Regression, Random Forest). A key aspect is the investigation of performance when models are trained on biased "convenience samples" that simulate non-response biases common in real-world survey research (e.g., training only on student data, data from a specific region, or data from unemployed participants). The authors found that fine-tuned LLMs, particularly smaller open-source models (3B-8B parameters), often outperform zero-shot LLMs and tabular methods in recovering individual predictions and population-level vote share distributions, even when trained on these biased samples.  They also show that under missing completely at random fine-tuned LLMs are comparable to tabular classifiers but significantly outperform zero-shot prompting.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its **application of LLMs to a practical survey research problem** (imputing missing data, specifically under systematic non-response bias), and its **focus on German survey data**.  Much existing work has concentrated on U.S. surveys and/or data that is missing completely at random.  The emphasis on *realistic* missingness scenarios through convenience samples is also a valuable contribution. While using LLMs for opinion prediction is not entirely new, the emphasis on German data, fine-tuning for *imputation* under *realistic bias conditions*, and the comparison to tabular models represent a worthwhile combination.

*   **Significance:**  The findings have significant potential implications for survey research.  If fine-tuned LLMs can effectively mitigate biases introduced by non-response or enable the use of cost-effective convenience samples, this could lead to more efficient and reliable survey designs. The demonstration that smaller, open-source LLMs can be effective is also practically important for researchers with limited access to resources. The study highlights a path for dealing with non-response bias a critical challenge for empirical social science. The results are relevant for empirical social science.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Experimental Design:**  The paper has a well-defined methodology, using stratified cross-validation and exploring several realistic missingness scenarios.
    *   **Comparison to Baselines:** The inclusion of zero-shot LLMs and various tabular classifiers provides a solid benchmark for assessing the performance of the fine-tuned LLMs.
    *   **Focus on Practical Scenarios:** The emphasis on systematic non-response and the use of convenience samples makes the research highly relevant to real-world survey challenges.
    *   **Open-Source Emphasis:**  The successful use of smaller, open-source LLMs is a plus for accessibility and reproducibility.
    *   **Clear Presentation of Results:** The figures and tables provide a clear and concise summary of the experimental outcomes.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study is limited to vote choice prediction in the GLES.  While a valuable starting point, it would be useful to see if the findings generalize to other survey variables, surveys in other countries, and to scenarios where open-ended responses are included. While there is a small section that states "for surveys that include open-ended questions.." this was not included in the results.
    *   **Small Training Data in Some Scenarios:** In some of the convenience sample scenarios (e.g., training only on unemployed participants), the amount of training data is very small (N=43), which limits the potential for fine-tuning and affects the reliability of the findings in those settings. The results show little impact, which isn't surprising given the lack of data for training.
    *   **Zero-Shot Prompting Simplifications:** The zero-shot baselines are limited by not exploring a broader prompt set for comparison. Acknowledging the possibility to improve upon their prompt engineering would solidify the approach.
    *   **Computational Costs:** While the paper mentions the accessibility of fine-tuning small models, a more detailed discussion of the computational costs involved would be beneficial, especially for researchers with limited resources.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper provides a promising direction for using LLMs to address critical issues in survey research.  If the findings are validated and extended by future work, this could significantly influence how surveys are designed and analyzed.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by applying LLMs to a relevant practical problem (survey imputation), under conditions of non-response bias commonly found in real-world surveys. The empirical validation, use of open-source models, and comparison with both zero-shot LLMs and tabular methods strengthen the paper's claims. However, the limitations (scope, small training data in some scenarios, zero-shot simplifications) prevent it from being a truly exceptional, groundbreaking contribution. I am giving this a high middle score because there were no new innovative concepts within the paper, it is a collection of concepts, but an important collection that bridges the gap between theoretical LLM capabilities to the empirical practices needed within social sciences.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### An empirical study on the limitation of Transformers in program trace generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25073v1)
- **Authors**: Simeng Sun
- **Abstract**: We study Transformers on the task \emph{program trace generation} (PTG), where models produce step-by-step execution traces for synthetic programs. Unlike existing algorithmic problems, PTG externalizes reasoning through long traces where each step is trivial. We train small Transformers with diverse modifications, including alternative position encodings, softmax replacements, hybrid model, and short convolutions. While these models achieve strong in-distribution accuracy, they exhibit systematic failures when generalizing to various factors (e.g., program length, trace steps), though some designs significantly improve generalization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the limitations of Transformers in program trace generation (PTG). PTG involves models generating step-by-step execution traces for synthetic programs, essentially externalizing reasoning into a long, structured trace. The authors train small Transformers with diverse modifications (position encodings, softmax replacements, hybrid models, convolutions) and evaluate their ability to generalize to unseen program lengths, trace steps, number of variables, and input sizes. While achieving strong in-distribution accuracy, the models struggle to generalize reliably, highlighting limitations in their ability to execute programs step-by-step, a critical aspect for reasoning tasks and instruction following.  The results show that architectural choices, particularly different positional encoding methods, have a significant impact on out-of-distribution performance, with NAPE (mixing NoPE and ALiBi heads) performing relatively better.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces PTG as a task, which is a clever way to dissect the reasoning capabilities of transformers into individual, verifiable steps. While synthetic program generation and using traces are not entirely novel concepts, framing the task as a direct evaluation of step-by-step execution is a useful contribution. The exploration of different Transformer modifications is less novel on its own, as many of these modifications have been studied in other contexts. However, their *application* to the PTG task and analysis of their impact on generalization is where the paper shows value.
*   **Significance:** The findings are significant because they expose vulnerabilities in Transformers' ability to reliably follow simple computational rules across longer sequences and varying program structures. This directly impacts the field's understanding of how well current models can perform reasoning tasks that require sequential application of simple operations, a crucial component of instruction following in LLMs. Identifying the importance of position embeddings and some successful variants helps guide future research. This research has significance in that we're trying to offload algorithmic processing to LLMs, this research shows where existing models need to improve in a particular area.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Formulation:** PTG is well-defined and provides a controllable environment for studying Transformer limitations.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The systematic investigation of several factors (program length, trace steps, variables, input size) provides a detailed picture of generalization performance.
    *   **Useful Ablation Studies:** Comparing various Transformer modifications, especially position encodings, offers insights into architectural choices.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors detail the data generation and model training procedures well, increasing the potential for other researchers to replicate and extend their work.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Model Scale:** Using "small" Transformers may not fully represent the capabilities of larger models, which might exhibit better generalization (though this also might simply be more expensive to research).
    *   **Synthetic Data:** While providing control, the synthetic nature of the programs might not perfectly reflect the complexities of real-world tasks, limiting the generalizability of the findings to more realistic problems.
    *   **Lack of Deeper Analysis:** While the paper identifies that *some* architectures perform better (NAPE), it doesn't delve deeply into *why* certain designs facilitate better generalization in this context. A deeper mechanistic understanding would have significantly increased the paper's impact.

**Overall:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by introducing PTG as a testbed for studying Transformer generalization, particularly concerning step-by-step execution. The empirical findings, though based on synthetic data, reveal important limitations of current models and highlight the influence of architectural choices. The lack of deep analysis on *why* some variants are better, and use of small model sizes, are limitations. Still, the clear problem statement, reproducible setup, and useful results justify a reasonable evaluation score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### UniLat3D: Geometry-Appearance Unified Latents for Single-Stage 3D Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25079v1)
- **Authors**: Guanjun Wu, Jiemin Fang, Chen Yang, Sikuang Li, Taoran Yi, Jia Lu, Zanwei Zhou, Jiazhong Cen, Lingxi Xie, Xiaopeng Zhang, Wei Wei, Wenyu Liu, Xinggang Wang, Qi Tian
- **Abstract**: High-fidelity 3D asset generation is crucial for various industries. While recent 3D pretrained models show strong capability in producing realistic content, most are built upon diffusion models and follow a two-stage pipeline that first generates geometry and then synthesizes appearance. Such a decoupled design tends to produce geometry-texture misalignment and non-negligible cost. In this paper, we propose UniLat3D, a unified framework that encodes geometry and appearance in a single latent space, enabling direct single-stage generation. Our key contribution is a geometry-appearance Unified VAE, which compresses high-resolution sparse features into a compact latent representation -- UniLat. UniLat integrates structural and visual information into a dense low-resolution latent, which can be efficiently decoded into diverse 3D formats, e.g., 3D Gaussians and meshes. Based on this unified representation, we train a single flow-matching model to map Gaussian noise directly into UniLat, eliminating redundant stages. Trained solely on public datasets, UniLat3D produces high-quality 3D assets in seconds from a single image, achieving superior appearance fidelity and geometric quality. More demos \& code are available at https://unilat3d.github.io/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "UniLat3D: Geometry-Appearance Unified Latents for Single-Stage 3D Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UniLat3D, a novel framework for generating 3D assets from single images in a single stage.  The key contribution is a geometry-appearance Unified VAE (UniVAE) that compresses high-resolution sparse features into a compact latent representation called UniLat. UniLat integrates structural and visual information into a dense, low-resolution latent space, which can be decoded into various 3D formats (3D Gaussians and meshes). A single flow-matching model is trained to map Gaussian noise directly into UniLat, eliminating the need for a two-stage generation process.  The method is trained solely on public datasets and demonstrates high-quality 3D asset generation in seconds. The authors emphasize improvements in appearance fidelity and geometric quality compared to existing approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the UniLat representation and its ability to enable single-stage 3D generation by unifying geometry and appearance in a single latent space. This departs from the common two-stage approach. The architecture details, while leveraging components from existing works like TRELLIS, are tailored to achieve this unification. The single flow-matching model trained to directly generate UniLat latents is another novel component.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential for increased efficiency and improved alignment between geometry and appearance. Two-stage methods can suffer from geometry-texture misalignment. UniLat3D promises to mitigate this, potentially leading to more realistic and visually appealing 3D assets.  The single-stage process also reduces computational overhead. The extensible nature of UniLat for integration into multimodal models is another potentially significant advantage. The achieved results on public datasets are promising.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and well-motivated approach.
    *   The unified latent space for geometry and appearance is a compelling idea.
    *   Single-stage generation pipeline enhances efficiency.
    *   Promising experimental results demonstrate good appearance fidelity and geometry quality, especially given the use of only public datasets.
    *   Thorough evaluation against state-of-the-art methods, and detailed ablation studies.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method still heavily relies on existing components like sparse Transformers from TRELLIS.
    *   While the results are impressive, the architectural differences from prior work might not be drastically different beyond the core concept of unification. This raises the question of how significant are the architectural changes, and how many parameters would need to be changed to achieve similar results on TRELLIS directly.
    *   The reliance on sparse features as an intermediate representation adds complexity to the pipeline. Is this representation the optimal one?
    *   Quantitative improvements over state-of-the-art methods, while present, are not always overwhelmingly superior.
    *   The experimental section mentions using a data processing pipeline following TRELLIS, which implies dependence on TRELLIS.
    *   Comparisons to commercial methods are qualitative and could be misleading.

*   **Potential Impact:** UniLat3D has the potential to influence future research in 3D generation by promoting unified representations and single-stage pipelines. Its ability to generate high-quality assets from single images quickly could have practical applications in various industries. Also its extensibility to multimodal learning has great potential.
*   **Room for improvement:** The efficiency and quality trade-off are promising, yet there is room to further improve results and scalability. Better exploration of the design choices and how they improve the results over baselines.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

UniLat3D represents a solid contribution with a clear goal, novel approach, and impressive experimental results. The single-stage unified latent space is a significant architectural contribution. However, the reliance on existing components and incremental improvements relative to state-of-the-art methods prevent it from reaching a higher score. While the idea is elegant and well-executed, it doesn't fundamentally revolutionize the field. A score of 7 reflects that it makes a valuable and original contribution that has the potential to influence future research. It solves geometry-appearance misalignment, an important issue in 3D asset generation, but the novelty is incremental. The method introduces a better approach to 3D generation, and the results are improved over baseline, but may not constitute a paradigm shift. It could be considered a significant advance within the limitations of the 3D asset generation field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards a Certificate of Trust: Task-Aware OOD Detection for Scientific AI
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25080v1)
- **Authors**: Bogdan Raonić, Siddhartha Mishra, Samuel Lanthaler
- **Abstract**: Data-driven models are increasingly adopted in critical scientific fields like weather forecasting and fluid dynamics. These methods can fail on out-of-distribution (OOD) data, but detecting such failures in regression tasks is an open challenge. We propose a new OOD detection method based on estimating joint likelihoods using a score-based diffusion model. This approach considers not just the input but also the regression model's prediction, providing a task-aware reliability score. Across numerous scientific datasets, including PDE datasets, satellite imagery and brain tumor segmentation, we show that this likelihood strongly correlates with prediction error. Our work provides a foundational step towards building a verifiable 'certificate of trust', thereby offering a practical tool for assessing the trustworthiness of AI-based scientific predictions. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/bogdanraonic3/OOD_Detection_ScientificML
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel method for out-of-distribution (OOD) detection in scientific AI regression tasks.  It proposes a "certificate of trust" based on estimating joint likelihoods using score-based diffusion models. This approach incorporates both the input data and the regression model's prediction to create a task-aware reliability score. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of this method across diverse scientific datasets, including PDE solutions, satellite imagery, and medical image segmentation, showing a strong correlation between the likelihood score and prediction error.  The authors claim their approach is zero-shot, requiring no ground truth predictions for OOD detection, and can also provide a posteriori error estimates when ground truth is available. The code is publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a relatively novel approach to OOD detection for regression tasks by leveraging score-based diffusion models for joint likelihood estimation of inputs and model predictions. This is a departure from traditional OOD detection methods more focused on classification or input-space density estimation. While diffusion models have been applied to OOD detection before (e.g., reconstruction-based methods), the joint likelihood approach tailored for regression seems comparatively unexplored. The use of the joint likelihood provides a "task-aware" element that is an improvement over methods focused purely on input distribution. However, the overall novelty is incremental as it builds on existing techniques (diffusion models, likelihood estimation) to address a specific problem (OOD for scientific regression).

*   **Significance:** OOD detection is a crucial problem for deploying machine learning in scientific applications, where failures can have significant consequences. Demonstrating a method that can improve reliability is important. The paper addresses this need by providing a tool to assess the trustworthiness of AI-based scientific predictions. The paper's focus on scientific applications gives it practical importance. The demonstration across diverse scientific datasets strengthens the argument for its broad applicability.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The core idea of using a joint likelihood estimated by a diffusion model is clever and addresses the task-awareness issue in OOD detection.
    *   The approach is empirically validated on a variety of relevant and challenging scientific datasets, showcasing the generalizability of the method.
    *   The zero-shot nature of the method is a significant advantage, making it practical for real-world scenarios where ground truth data is often unavailable.
    *   The availability of code promotes reproducibility and further research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The theoretical justification, while providing some intuition, is based on approximations and a heuristic relation, which might not always hold.
    *   The results are primarily empirical. A more rigorous analysis of why this method works and its limitations would improve the paper.
    *   The paper does not explore the computational cost of training the diffusion model, evaluating the joint likelihood with the flow ODE, or comparing to lighter-weight alternatives. It's important to understand if this method adds significant overhead compared to alternatives.
    *   While the authors adapt other certificates derived from the probability-flow ODE, a comparison against a wider range of OOD detection baselines would further strengthen their contributions.
    *  The "zero-shot" capability is partially misleading, as the method still needs a small set of "decision samples" from the training distribution to determine the threshold. The sensitivity analysis of this decision sample should be further explored.
    * Some parts of the supplementary materials should be included in the main paper to support the contributions.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of scientific machine learning by providing a practical tool for assessing prediction reliability. It could encourage wider adoption of data-driven models in critical scientific fields, where trust and safety are paramount. It provides a strong baseline for future research on verifiable AI in scientific computing.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7.

The paper presents a valuable contribution with a well-motivated and empirically supported method. The "task-aware" aspect of the OOD detection, the practical demonstration across scientific datasets, and the zero-shot nature are significant strengths. However, the paper lacks deeper theoretical analysis, more rigorous comparison to baselines, a discussion on computational cost, and further clarification on the "zero-shot" nature.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### MANI-Pure: Magnitude-Adaptive Noise Injection for Adversarial Purification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25082v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoyi Huang, Junwei Wu, Kejia Zhang, Carl Yang, Zhiming Luo
- **Abstract**: Adversarial purification with diffusion models has emerged as a promising defense strategy, but existing methods typically rely on uniform noise injection, which indiscriminately perturbs all frequencies, corrupting semantic structures and undermining robustness. Our empirical study reveals that adversarial perturbations are not uniformly distributed: they are predominantly concentrated in high-frequency regions, with heterogeneous magnitude intensity patterns that vary across frequencies and attack types. Motivated by this observation, we introduce MANI-Pure, a magnitude-adaptive purification framework that leverages the magnitude spectrum of inputs to guide the purification process. Instead of injecting homogeneous noise, MANI-Pure adaptively applies heterogeneous, frequency-targeted noise, effectively suppressing adversarial perturbations in fragile high-frequency, low-magnitude bands while preserving semantically critical low-frequency content. Extensive experiments on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet-1K validate the effectiveness of MANI-Pure. It narrows the clean accuracy gap to within 0.59 of the original classifier, while boosting robust accuracy by 2.15, and achieves the top-1 robust accuracy on the RobustBench leaderboard, surpassing the previous state-of-the-art method.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MANI-PURE: MAGNITUDE-ADAPTIVE NOISE INJECTION FOR ADVERSARIAL PURIFICATION":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MANI-Pure, a novel adversarial purification framework that improves the robustness of deep neural networks against adversarial attacks. MANI-Pure's core idea is to inject noise adaptively based on the magnitude spectrum of the input image. This approach contrasts with existing diffusion-based purification methods that inject uniform noise, which can corrupt semantic information. MANI-Pure uses frequency-targeted noise injection, focusing on high-frequency, low-magnitude bands where adversarial perturbations are more concentrated, while preserving semantically important low-frequency content. The framework includes two key modules: Magnitude-Adaptive Noise Injection (MANI) and Frequency Purification (FreqPure). MANI dynamically adjusts noise intensity based on the magnitude spectrum, and FreqPure employs magnitude-phase decomposition to distinguish and preserve low-frequency components. Experimental results on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet-1K demonstrate that MANI-Pure achieves state-of-the-art robust accuracy while maintaining high clean accuracy, outperforming existing diffusion-based purification methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its magnitude-adaptive approach to noise injection.  The observation that adversarial perturbations are not uniformly distributed across the frequency domain is not entirely new, but MANI-Pure leverages this insight to create a more targeted purification process. The combination of MANI and FreqPure is also a novel architectural contribution, and the authors demonstrate the effectiveness of both components in ablation studies. Compared to other methods that utilize specific frequency information, MANI appears to use information about frequency intensities in a more adaptive, data driven, rather than handcrafted way.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper is substantial. Adversarial robustness remains a critical challenge in deep learning, particularly for safety-critical applications. MANI-Pure represents a valuable contribution to the field of adversarial purification by demonstrating a practical and effective approach to improve robustness without significant degradation in clean accuracy.  The fact that it achieves state-of-the-art performance on RobustBench and is demonstrated to be "plug-and-play" significantly increases its potential adoption and impact. The modular design could allow easier integration with other existing defense techniques.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong Empirical Results: Extensive experiments show the effectiveness of MANI-Pure against diverse and adaptive attacks on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet-1K.
    *   Principled Approach: The framework is well-motivated by the analysis of adversarial perturbations in the frequency domain.
    *   Plug-and-Play Compatibility: MANI-Pure can be easily integrated with existing diffusion-based purification methods.
    *   Clear Presentation: The paper is well-written and clearly explains the framework and its benefits.
    *   Good Ablation Study: The ablation studies provide insight into the importance of each component of MANI-Pure.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Computational Cost: While claimed to be relatively small, a detailed analysis of the added computational overhead due to DFT/IDFT operations would be beneficial. The manuscript only claims it negligible compared to existing denoising diffusion costs.
    *   Generality: The experiments focus on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet-1K. Testing on other datasets or tasks (e.g., object detection) would strengthen the claim of generalizability. The limited datasets is a significant issue.
    *   The analysis relies on radial spectrum analysis which can obscure directional information that may also play a role.

*   **Potential Influence:** MANI-Pure has the potential to influence future research in adversarial defense by promoting the use of adaptive and frequency-aware purification techniques. The plug-and-play nature could lead to its incorporation into existing defense pipelines and may spur further exploration of hybrid defense strategies. The core idea could be extended to other domains beyond image classification.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the significant improvements in robust accuracy, the well-motivated design, the clear presentation, and plug and play compatibility, I rate this work with a Score: 7.5.

The potential issues about analysis reliance on radial spectrum, computational cost and dataset generality, are important, preventing a higher score. The work is strong in its ability to present substantial empirical results showing significant improvements in robust and clean accuracy, a solid approach for designing the proposed architecture, well written structure and the fact that the proposed architecture is designed to be plug-and-play with other existing techniques, which could lead to further advancements in hybrid approaches. The proposed architecture is novel, useful, well tested and well presented.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Trustworthy Lexical Simplification: Exploring Safety and Efficiency with Small LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25086v1)
- **Authors**: Akio Hayakawa, Stefan Bott, Horacio Saggion
- **Abstract**: Despite their strong performance, large language models (LLMs) face challenges in real-world application of lexical simplification (LS), particularly in privacy-sensitive and resource-constrained environments. Moreover, since vulnerable user groups (e.g., people with disabilities) are one of the key target groups of this technology, it is crucial to ensure the safety and correctness of the output of LS systems. To address these issues, we propose an efficient framework for LS systems that utilizes small LLMs deployable in local environments. Within this framework, we explore knowledge distillation with synthesized data and in-context learning as baselines. Our experiments in five languages evaluate model outputs both automatically and manually. Our manual analysis reveals that while knowledge distillation boosts automatic metric scores, it also introduces a safety trade-off by increasing harmful simplifications. Importantly, we find that the model's output probability is a useful signal for detecting harmful simplifications. Leveraging this, we propose a filtering strategy that suppresses harmful simplifications while largely preserving beneficial ones. This work establishes a benchmark for efficient and safe LS with small LLMs. It highlights the key trade-offs between performance, efficiency, and safety, and demonstrates a promising approach for safe real-world deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Towards Trustworthy Lexical Simplification: Exploring Safety and Efficiency with Small LLMs":

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the use of small language models (LLMs) for lexical simplification (LS) in resource-constrained and privacy-sensitive settings. The authors focus on balancing efficiency, safety, and performance in LS systems. They explore two approaches: in-context learning and knowledge distillation from a larger teacher model. Through experiments in five languages, they evaluate the generated simplifications automatically and manually, focusing on identifying and mitigating harmful simplifications (e.g., grammatical errors, meaning changes, increased difficulty). The key finding is that while knowledge distillation improves automatic metrics, it can also increase the risk of harmful outputs. They propose and evaluate a filtering strategy based on the model's output probability as a signal for detecting and suppressing these harmful simplifications. The paper establishes a benchmark for efficient and safe LS using small LLMs and highlights the trade-offs between performance, efficiency, and safety in this context.

**Critical Evaluation:**
