*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the systematic exploration of small LLMs for LS with a specific focus on safety and efficiency, especially regarding potentially vulnerable user groups. While using LLMs for LS is not new, the explicit investigation of the trade-offs, particularly the introduction of a filtering strategy guided by output probability for enhanced safety, is a useful contribution. The multi-lingual approach and manual analysis also add value.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge: deploying LS systems in real-world scenarios where privacy and resource constraints are paramount. The study's finding that standard knowledge distillation techniques can inadvertently increase harmful outputs underscores the need for careful consideration of safety when working with small LLMs. The proposed filtering strategy, even if preliminary, provides a promising direction for developing more trustworthy LS systems. The multi-lingual experiments and the establishment of a baseline further enhance the significance of the work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of using LLMs for LS, including privacy concerns and resource limitations.
    *   **Focus on Safety:** The explicit focus on safety, especially in the context of vulnerable user groups, is a major strength.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The combination of automatic metrics and manual analysis provides a comprehensive evaluation of the LS systems.
    *   **Multi-Lingual Approach:** The experiments across five languages add generality and broaden the impact of the findings.
    *   **Output probability filtering strategy:** This looks at the probability and tries to avoid simplifying when the probability output is low, this can help prevent inaccurate simplifications from occuring.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses only on lexical simplification and does not address more complex sentence or document-level simplification tasks.
    *   **Simple Filtering Strategy:** While promising, the proposed filtering strategy based on output probability is relatively simple and may not be sufficient to address all types of harmful simplifications. Its efficacy hinges on the quality of the output probability scores.
    *   **Small Model Size:** The small model sizes might limit the generalizability of the results to larger, more powerful LLMs.
    *   **Single Annotator:** The reliance on a single annotator in manual evaluation raises concerns about subjectivity and the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **No ablation study on prompting strategies:** The fixed few-shot prompting strategy lacks investigation into alternative prompt formulations.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper sets a valuable benchmark and motivates further research on efficient and safe LS with small LLMs. The filtering strategy based on output probability can inspire more sophisticated methods for ensuring the reliability and trustworthiness of LS systems in real-world applications. It also stimulates interest in safety-aware training techniques.

*   **Justification of Score:** While the work contributes meaningfully to the field and addresses critical challenges in LS, the limited scope, simple filtering strategy, model sizes, and reliance on single annotators somewhat temper the impact. The explicit focus on safety, multi-lingual experiments, and potential influence of the findings justify a solid but not exceptional score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Knowledge Extraction on Semi-Structured Content: Does It Remain Relevant for Question Answering in the Era of LLMs?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25107v1)
- **Authors**: Kai Sun, Yin Huang, Srishti Mehra, Mohammad Kachuee, Xilun Chen, Renjie Tao, Zhaojiang Lin, Andrea Jessee, Nirav Shah, Alex Betty, Yue Liu, Anuj Kumar, Wen-tau Yih, Xin Luna Dong
- **Abstract**: The advent of Large Language Models (LLMs) has significantly advanced web-based Question Answering (QA) systems over semi-structured content, raising questions about the continued utility of knowledge extraction for question answering. This paper investigates the value of triple extraction in this new paradigm by extending an existing benchmark with knowledge extraction annotations and evaluating commercial and open-source LLMs of varying sizes. Our results show that web-scale knowledge extraction remains a challenging task for LLMs. Despite achieving high QA accuracy, LLMs can still benefit from knowledge extraction, through augmentation with extracted triples and multi-task learning. These findings provide insights into the evolving role of knowledge triple extraction in web-based QA and highlight strategies for maximizing LLM effectiveness across different model sizes and resource settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Knowledge Extraction on Semi-Structured Content: Does It Remain Relevant for Question Answering in the Era of LLMs?".

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the utility of knowledge extraction (specifically, triple extraction) from semi-structured web content for question answering (QA), especially in light of the advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors extend the WebSRC benchmark with knowledge extraction annotations and evaluate several commercial and open-source LLMs.  Their results indicate that while LLMs achieve high QA accuracy, especially on cleaned data, they still benefit from knowledge extraction, both through direct augmentation with extracted triples and through multi-task learning that explicitly trains the LLM on triple extraction. They also explore a script-based approach to knowledge extraction, aiming for a more efficient alternative to running LLMs directly on every webpage. The study ultimately suggests that knowledge extraction remains a valuable component for improving LLMs' QA performance, particularly for smaller models and noisy, real-world webpages.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core question of whether explicit knowledge extraction remains relevant in the LLM era is timely and pertinent. Evaluating this on semi-structured content is also a good choice, as it presents a unique domain. The extension of the WebSRC benchmark with knowledge extraction annotations is a valuable contribution in itself. The script-based extraction approach is also reasonably novel. However, the findings themselves are somewhat predictable, albeit still useful. The general conclusion that LLMs benefit from additional knowledge is not groundbreaking.

*   **Significance:**  The paper's significance lies primarily in its empirical evaluation and practical insights. It offers a quantitative assessment of how knowledge extraction can complement LLMs for web-based QA. The findings that smaller models benefit more and that certain techniques work better on noisier data are valuable for practitioners. Furthermore, the script-based extraction offers a potentially crucial pathway for scaling knowledge extraction for very large web applications.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper features a wide range of experiments, using diverse LLMs and evaluation metrics.
    *   **Well-Defined Research Questions:**  The research questions are clearly articulated and addressed systematically.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The findings have practical implications for designing and optimizing web-based QA systems that incorporate LLMs.
    *   **Dataset Contribution:** The extension of WebSRC increases the value of that benchmark for future research.
    *   **Script-based Approach:** The investigation of a script-based extraction method is promising and aligns with the necessity for more cost-effective knowledge retrieval methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Findings:** While the experiments are solid, the overall conclusions are not significantly surprising. The idea that explicit knowledge helps LLMs, even powerful ones, is a theme seen in many papers now.
    *   **Limited Generalizability of Scripts:** The success of script-based knowledge extraction is promising, but the difficulties encountered with whole webpages suggests the scripts may be too specific to the cleaned training examples and thus lacks the required generalization ability.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Error Analysis:**  More detail regarding where extraction and QA fail despite the presented methods would add value. Are there particular types of knowledge difficult to extract? Are some QA paradigms still not addressable even after KE?

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of web-based QA systems that rely on LLMs, especially in resource-constrained settings. It encourages a hybrid approach that combines the strengths of LLMs with the structured knowledge derived from knowledge extraction.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** to this paper. While the paper explores a valuable and current research direction, its findings are more incremental than revolutionary. The extension to the WebSRC dataset is commendable. The comprehensive experimental setup provides robust empirical evidence, and the practical insights are useful. The most interesting and potentially high-impact contribution is the script-based extraction, but this is also the area with the most limitations currently. The paper's significance lies in its providing solid quantitative support for incorporating knowledge extraction techniques in LLM-driven QA systems, offering valuable guidance for practical applications. However, it falls short of making truly novel theoretical advancements or demonstrating a solution to a particularly hard unsolved challenge.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Score Distillation of Flow Matching Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25127v1)
- **Authors**: Mingyuan Zhou, Yi Gu, Huangjie Zheng, Liangchen Song, Guande He, Yizhe Zhang, Wenze Hu, Yinfei Yang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models achieve high-quality image generation but are limited by slow iterative sampling. Distillation methods alleviate this by enabling one- or few-step generation. Flow matching, originally introduced as a distinct framework, has since been shown to be theoretically equivalent to diffusion under Gaussian assumptions, raising the question of whether distillation techniques such as score distillation transfer directly. We provide a simple derivation -- based on Bayes' rule and conditional expectations -- that unifies Gaussian diffusion and flow matching without relying on ODE/SDE formulations. Building on this view, we extend Score identity Distillation (SiD) to pretrained text-to-image flow-matching models, including SANA, SD3-Medium, SD3.5-Medium/Large, and FLUX.1-dev, all with DiT backbones. Experiments show that, with only modest flow-matching- and DiT-specific adjustments, SiD works out of the box across these models, in both data-free and data-aided settings, without requiring teacher finetuning or architectural changes. This provides the first systematic evidence that score distillation applies broadly to text-to-image flow matching models, resolving prior concerns about stability and soundness and unifying acceleration techniques across diffusion- and flow-based generators. We will make the PyTorch implementation publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Score Distillation of Flow Matching Models":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of slow sampling in high-quality image generation models, particularly diffusion models. It focuses on applying distillation techniques, specifically Score identity Distillation (SiD), to accelerate flow matching models, which are theoretically equivalent to diffusion models under Gaussian assumptions but are often treated as a distinct framework. The authors provide a unified derivation of diffusion and flow matching based on Bayes' rule and conditional expectations, removing the need for ODE/SDE formulations. They then extend SiD to pre-trained text-to-image flow-matching models (SANA, SD3-MEDIUM/LARGE, FLUX.1-DEV with DiT backbones).  Their experiments show that, with some flow-matching/DiT-specific adjustments, SiD works effectively across these models, both in data-free and data-aided settings, without extensive finetuning or architectural changes. The paper unifies acceleration techniques across diffusion- and flow-based generators and provides a PyTorch implementation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The primary novelty lies in demonstrating the direct applicability of score distillation, specifically SiD, to flow-matching models *without extensive adaptation*. Previous works like SANA-Sprint required significant finetuning and adjustments for different architectures. The paper's claim to achieving this with minimal modifications is a strong point. The unification of diffusion and flow-matching through a simplified derivation is not entirely new (many papers make this theoretical connection), but the way the authors present it as a practical justification for the success of SiD is valuable.
*   **Significance:**  The paper's significance lies in bridging the gap between diffusion and flow-matching distillation techniques. If the method indeed works "out of the box" across different flow-matching models, it saves considerable effort in developing bespoke distillation pipelines for each model. Furthermore, the paper addresses concerns surrounding the stability and soundness of score distillation when applied to flow matching. The successful application to models like FLUX.1-DEV is also important, as it showcases applicability to models with significant architectural differences. The availability of code is important for replicability and follow-on research.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides a sound theoretical grounding, avoiding SDE/ODE formulations which is helpful for practitioners.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The empirical results, particularly showing good performance on a variety of flow-matching models (SANA, SD3 variants, and FLUX), support the claims well.
    *   **Practicality:** The promise of a single codebase and hyperparameter configuration underscores the ease of adoption.
    *   **Addresses concerns:** Paper directly addresses concerns raised about stability and applicability in previous work.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Analysis on Architecture-Specific Adjustments:** While the paper claims "modest adjustments," the details and rationale behind these adjustments could be fleshed out more clearly.
    *   **Adversarial Training Data:** The use of MidJourney data, while done for reproducibility, is a potential source of criticism. More importantly, the discussion of limitations of the synthetic dataset, while appreciated, may raise questions about the quality of SiD2. While it shows a benefit, the exact gain, and whether real data is better would strengthen the conclusions.
    *   **Dependence on DiT Architecture:** The method seems heavily reliant on the Diffusion Transformer (DiT) architecture. While DiT is popular, it limits the generality of the finding. A discussion on the limitations stemming from relying on DiT backbones would be helpful.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution by demonstrating the transferability of score distillation techniques to flow-matching models with minimal architecture specific adaptations. While the theoretical connections between diffusion and flow matching have been established, this paper provides a compelling empirical demonstration of how to accelerate these methods with shared distillation tooling.  It simplifies and unifies the landscape of generative modeling acceleration, making it easier for researchers and practitioners to compress pre-trained flow matching T2I generators with SiD.

**Score: 7.5**

The paper demonstrates a novel contribution to the field of generative modeling, while the method is still relatively new and could benefit from further work, such as improving the method to models with different architectures, and analyzing how specific characteristics of different types of flow matching models affect the quality of score identity distillation, this work addresses a need within the field, that will be beneficial to future research in this domain.
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### BALF: Budgeted Activation-Aware Low-Rank Factorization for Fine-Tuning-Free Model Compression
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25136v1)
- **Authors**: David González Martínez
- **Abstract**: Neural network compression techniques typically require expensive fine-tuning or search procedures, rendering them impractical on commodity hardware. Inspired by recent LLM compression research, we present a general activation-aware factorization framework that can be applied to a broad range of layers. Moreover, we introduce a scalable budgeted rank allocator that allows flexible control over compression targets (e.g., retaining 50% of parameters) with no overhead. Together, these components form BALF, an efficient pipeline for compressing models without fine-tuning. We demonstrate its effectiveness across multiple scales and architectures, from ResNet-20 on CIFAR-10 to ResNeXt-101 and vision transformers on ImageNet, and show that it achieves excellent results in the fine-tuning-free regime. For instance, BALF reduces FLOPs on ResNeXt-101 by 45% with only a 1-percentage-point top-1 accuracy drop.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "BALF: BUDGETED ACTIVATION-AWARE LOW-RANK FACTORIZATION FOR FINE-TUNING-FREE MODEL COMPRESSION":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BALF (Budgeted Activation-Aware Low-Rank Factorization), a novel framework for compressing neural networks without requiring expensive fine-tuning. It builds upon activation-aware factorization techniques, particularly those used in LLM compression, and extends them to a broader range of layers, including convolutional layers. A key contribution is a scalable budgeted rank allocator, which uses Lagrangian relaxation to efficiently determine the compression ratio for each layer based on user-specified FLOPs or parameter count budgets. The authors demonstrate BALF's effectiveness across various models (ResNet, ViT, MobileNet) and datasets (CIFAR-10, ImageNet), achieving strong accuracy-compression trade-offs in the fine-tuning-free setting.  The method is shown to be practical, running quickly on commodity hardware and avoiding extensive hyperparameter tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper combines several existing ideas but integrates them in a novel and useful way. Activation-aware factorization has been explored primarily in LLMs, and this work's extension to CNNs and a more general class of layers is a valuable contribution. The budgeted rank allocation using Lagrangian relaxation is also a significant advance. While energy-based selection has been used, and multiple-choice knapsack problems are known, solving this efficiently and scalably within a compression framework adds to the value. The unified framework is novel as it combines and extends existing ideas.

* **Significance:** The ability to compress models effectively *without* fine-tuning is highly significant, especially in resource-constrained environments. The method's practicality (speed, minimal hyperparameter tuning) makes it much more accessible than many compression techniques. Achieving good accuracy with significant compression, demonstrated across a variety of architectures and datasets, further enhances the significance. BALF can improve the practicality of deploying larger models or allow for model deployment on resource-constrained devices.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Generality:**  BALF isn't limited to specific architectures or layer types, making it widely applicable.
    * **Efficiency:** Fast compression times and avoidance of expensive search procedures are major advantages.
    * **Practicality:** Designed for use on commodity hardware and minimal hyperparameter tuning.
    * **Strong Results:**  The empirical evaluation showcases impressive accuracy-compression trade-offs on multiple datasets.
    * **Budget-Aware Allocation:** Offers fine-grained control over compression targets.
    * **Principled Approach:** Uses Lagrangian relaxation for efficient rank allocation.
    * **Provides bounds:** A bound on the error induced by the compression method is provided.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Specialized Operators:** The authors acknowledge that specialized operator implementations could further improve speedups, a limitation common to many structured compression methods. This implies that the speedups observed might not be the upper bound. While the results are strong for a generic implementation, the reliance on more complex and specialized implementations could make practical adoption more difficult.
    * **Bound Quality:** It is also admitted that the theoretical error bounds are loose and might not be of use practically.
    * **MobileNet-V2:** Compress compact models (like MobileNet-V2) was still challenging.

* **Potential Influence:** BALF has the potential to become a widely used model compression technique. Its practicality and effectiveness make it a strong candidate for adoption in various applications. Future research could focus on developing specialized operators for even greater speedups and exploring combinations with other compression methods like quantization.

* **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a well-engineered solution to a significant problem (model compression). It is more practical and accessible than many existing techniques. The results are compelling. The integration of several existing ideas combined with the novel rank allocator warrants a high score. However, because it builds on existing research and specialized operator needs improvements and faces difficulties compressing very compact models, it's not ground-breaking in the way some papers can be.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Investigating Language and Retrieval Bias in Multilingual Previously Fact-Checked Claim Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25138v1)
- **Authors**: Ivan Vykopal, Antonia Karamolegkou, Jaroslav Kopčan, Qiwei Peng, Tomáš Javůrek, Michal Gregor, Marián Šimko
- **Abstract**: Multilingual Large Language Models (LLMs) offer powerful capabilities for cross-lingual fact-checking. However, these models often exhibit language bias, performing disproportionately better on high-resource languages such as English than on low-resource counterparts. We also present and inspect a novel concept - retrieval bias, when information retrieval systems tend to favor certain information over others, leaving the retrieval process skewed. In this paper, we study language and retrieval bias in the context of Previously Fact-Checked Claim Detection (PFCD). We evaluate six open-source multilingual LLMs across 20 languages using a fully multilingual prompting strategy, leveraging the AMC-16K dataset. By translating task prompts into each language, we uncover disparities in monolingual and cross-lingual performance and identify key trends based on model family, size, and prompting strategy. Our findings highlight persistent bias in LLM behavior and offer recommendations for improving equity in multilingual fact-checking. To investigate retrieval bias, we employed multilingual embedding models and look into the frequency of retrieved claims. Our analysis reveals that certain claims are retrieved disproportionately across different posts, leading to inflated retrieval performance for popular claims while under-representing less common ones.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper, focusing on its novelty and significance:

**Summary**

The paper investigates language and retrieval bias in multilingual previously fact-checked claim detection (PFCD).  The authors evaluate several open-source multilingual LLMs (Qwen3, Llama3, Gemma) across 20 languages using a fully multilingual prompting strategy on the AMC-16K dataset.  They analyze monolingual and cross-lingual performance, identifying trends related to model family, size, and prompting strategies, highlighting persistent bias in LLM behavior.  Further, they introduce and explore the concept of "retrieval bias," where certain fact-checked claims are retrieved more frequently than others, regardless of actual relevance.  The authors measure retrieval frequency and employ topic modeling to uncover thematic patterns influencing retrieval bias. Finally they provide recommendations for improving equity in multilingual fact-checking.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper has several novel aspects:

    *   *Multilingual Prompting*: The authors move beyond English-centric prompting by translating prompts into all target languages. While not entirely groundbreaking, this is a methodological improvement over many existing studies and a contribution in itself.
    *   *Retrieval Bias Concept*: The explicit identification and analysis of "retrieval bias" in PFCD is a key contribution.  While the underlying mechanisms (popularity bias, data imbalance, etc.) are known in other domains like recommendation systems, framing it within the fact-checking context is novel and important. No explicit analysis of Retrieval Bias has been performed in similar tasks before. The connection the authors establish between retrieval bias in information retrieval to popularity bias in recommender systems is well-reasoned.
    *   *Comprehensive Evaluation*:  The breadth of the evaluation, covering multiple LLMs, 20 languages, various prompting strategies, and the analysis of both language *and* retrieval bias, gives the paper a depth that many studies lack.

*   **Significance and Impact:**

    *   *Highlighting Equity Concerns*: The work underscores the importance of addressing bias in multilingual fact-checking to ensure equitable access to trustworthy information.  This is particularly critical given the potential for misinformation to disproportionately affect vulnerable populations.
    *   *Practical Recommendations*: The paper offers practical recommendations for mitigating language and retrieval biases, such as enhanced prompting strategies and LLM-based reranking, which provides an actionable contribution.
    *   *Future Research Directions*: The explicit formalization of the concept of Retrieval bias in PFCD opens the door for future research into mitigation strategies and more comprehensive evaluation of this type of bias.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   *Methodological Rigor*: The systematic experimental design and statistical analysis strengthen the paper's conclusions.
    *   *Clear Problem Formulation*: The paper clearly defines the problem of bias and its implications for multilingual fact-checking.
    *   *Actionable Insights*: The findings provide concrete insights for researchers and practitioners working on PFCD systems.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   *Limited Scope of LLMs*: Focusing solely on open-source LLMs, while promoting reproducibility, potentially limits the generalizability of the findings to closed-source models such as GPT-4.
    *   *Limited mitigation efforts:* The mitigation efforts in the paper are limited to reranking and prompting strategies. There are several known mitigation strategies for popularity bias in recommender systems that could be adapted and explored within the fact-checking domain.
    *   *Superficial topic analysis:* While the topic analysis is a step in the right direction, a more robust analysis would allow us to quantify topical bias more accurately.
    *   *Dataset Limitations*: The inherent limitations of the AMC-16K dataset (task equivalence across languages, under-representation of certain regions) also affect the study.

*   **Justification for Score:**

The paper represents a solid contribution to the field of multilingual fact-checking. It introduces and analyzes the important concept of retrieval bias while also taking steps towards a more comprehensive evaluation framework accounting for both language and retrieval biases. The methodological approach is thorough, and the findings have practical implications for improving the fairness and effectiveness of PFCD systems. While there are limitations, particularly concerning the scope of models and analyses, the paper's novelty and its potential to stimulate further research in this critical area justify a high score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Vision-and-Language Navigation with Analogical Textual Descriptions in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25139v1)
- **Authors**: Yue Zhang, Tianyi Ma, Zun Wang, Yanyuan Qiao, Parisa Kordjamshidi
- **Abstract**: Integrating large language models (LLMs) into embodied AI models is becoming increasingly prevalent. However, existing zero-shot LLM-based Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) agents either encode images as textual scene descriptions, potentially oversimplifying visual details, or process raw image inputs, which can fail to capture abstract semantics required for high-level reasoning. In this paper, we improve the navigation agent's contextual understanding by incorporating textual descriptions from multiple perspectives that facilitate analogical reasoning across images. By leveraging text-based analogical reasoning, the agent enhances its global scene understanding and spatial reasoning, leading to more accurate action decisions. We evaluate our approach on the R2R dataset, where our experiments demonstrate significant improvements in navigation performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses limitations in current LLM-based Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) agents, specifically their difficulty in distinguishing visually similar scenes and their limited spatial understanding. It proposes an "analogical reasoning module" that enhances the agent's contextual understanding by:

1.  **Analogical Scene Descriptions:** Generating detailed visual descriptions that highlight distinguishing features between different viewpoint images, rather than describing each image independently.
2.  **Spatial Descriptions:** Constructing structured contextual representations that capture spatial relationships across discrete images, emphasizing directional comparisons, elevation differences, and distance variations.

The method prompts LLMs to generate descriptions that explicitly compare images, focusing on differences. The agent then uses these descriptions, along with images, history, and topological maps, to make navigation decisions. The paper evaluates the approach on the R2R and REVERIE datasets, demonstrating improved navigation performance compared to using raw text, images, or existing LLM-based methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the application of analogical reasoning to VLN, specifically in how visual scene descriptions are generated and used. Prompting the LLM to *compare* visual inputs and highlight *differences* to create a more contextual understanding is a meaningful step beyond directly describing each image independently.  The use of structured spatial descriptions that incorporate directional and distance information is also a worthwhile contribution. However, analogical reasoning itself is not a new concept within AI or NLP. The novelty is in how it is applied to this specific VLN problem. The paper also uses existing VLM and LLM models, so no new architecture in VLMs or LLMs is introduced.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a key challenge in LLM-based VLN: distinguishing visually similar scenes and understanding spatial relationships. The performance improvements demonstrated on R2R and REVERIE, while modest, are significant, indicating that the approach is effective. The ablation studies provide insights into the contributions of scene and spatial descriptions. This contribution will influence how future VLN agent designs consider using LLMs more effectively. Specifically, prompting strategies that incorporate relationship between the image set.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the limitations of current LLM-based VLN agents.
*   **Well-Defined Approach:** The analogical reasoning module is well-defined and easy to understand.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments are comprehensive, with ablation studies providing insights into the different components.
*   **Qualitative Examples:** The qualitative examples effectively illustrate how the analogical descriptions help the agent make better decisions.
*   **Reproducibility**: The paper provides code link and implementation details increasing the chances of being easily reproduced.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Modest Improvements:** While statistically significant, the performance gains might be considered modest. This could raise questions about the practical impact of the approach in real-world scenarios.
*   **Reliance on LLM Quality:** The performance is heavily reliant on the quality of the underlying LLMs, which are known to be sensitive to prompt engineering and may exhibit biases or hallucinations.
*   **Computational Cost:**  Generating the analogical descriptions adds computational overhead, which could be a concern for real-time applications.
*   **Limited novelty**. The paper builds on existing techniques and doesn't introduce significant theoretical breakthroughs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of VLN. While the performance improvements are not dramatic, they are significant and achieved through a well-designed and validated approach. The novelty is moderate, as it primarily involves applying existing analogical reasoning techniques to a new problem domain.  The paper addresses a clear limitation and provides valuable insights into how to improve LLM-based VLN agents. However, the dependence on LLMs quality and the relatively modest performance gains prevents it from reaching a higher score. It also heavily uses other model (VLM and LLM) which also contributes to decrease the score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10
